SIMPIEX ENGINEERING CORPORATION

DETROIT, MICHIGAN
July 14, 1941
Senator Prentiss M. Brown
United States Senate
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator Brown:
Thanks very much for your letter of July 9th. We
appreciate your efforts. Also the enclosure from the
State Department, who evidently has the authority, all
right, to stop these shipments, but is not doing much
about it. We are quoting you below from "The Detroit
News":
"San Pedro, Calif., July 1Oth
-Japanese vessels are taking more than 400,000 barrels
of oil from Los Angeles Harbor this week. The auxiliary
naval tanker Kokuyo Maru was due to sail today with
70,000 barrels, and the naval tanker Iro was completing
her cargo of 54,000 barrels.
The former whaler Tonan Maru III and the Kyokuyo
Maru are to sail Friday with 140,000 barrels each."
This looks very bad to us, especially in view of the
fact that Mr. Ickes is talking about gasless Sunday's.
For your further information, when we came through
Chicago after making a trip out West to California the
last two or three weeks, last Sunday's "Chicago Tribune"
carried the story of a large shipment of expensive aluminum
toys just arriving at one of the large Department Stores
from England. It pointed out the fact that manufacturers
in this country are unable to get aluminum for the same
type of toy; yet we are shipping aluminum to England and
they in turn manufacturing it and shipping it back here to
compete with our market.
We also have from good authority that the English
have delivered airplanes for commercial and private use
to South America and are soliciting that type of business
at the present time.
What

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
What I am trying to do is find out who we are
fighting for and what it is all about. It is a "Merry-go-
round" all right, and running at pretty high speed, and I
am wondering how long I can still hold on.
We thank you for your efforts, and remain
Yours truly,
DALE O. MILLER

HIFNENTS OF CHILEAN COPPER TO JAPAN
Corporation, National Zino Company, Inc., 1l Broadway
New York, New York.
OFFICE OF G AIVISER ON
F120
INIERNUNAI OC AFFA
JUL 23 1941A
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
894
894,24/1467
In the absence of Mr. Finletter, who had written to Mr. Elkan
requesting that he come to Washington to discuss a contract for
1563
the sale of blister copper to Japan, the latter came to my office
to go over the matter. The Embassy in Chile had informed the
Department in a despatoh dated June 23, 1941 (no. 1593) that
the International Minerals & Metals Corporation, which was under-
stood to have a contract with the Compagnie Miniere du M'Zaita
of Chile to take its entire production, had agreed to allow the
latter to sell 1600 tons of blister copper to Japan during the
romainder of this year.
which essentially is that of an agent, in the handling of copper
Th

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The International Minerals & Metals Corporation acts in this
capacity for Phelps Dodge and, as indicated by the despatch
from Chile, it has a contract for the entire output of
M'Zaita. M'Zaita operates a refinery utilizing its own
ores, but in addition it buys a certain quantity of other
ores necessary for use as a flux from small miners. The
output of M'Zaita is about 8o0 tons of blister copper per
month.
M'Zaita found, according to Mr. Elkan, that the Japanese
 competing with it for the purchase of certain ores not
controlled by the company but necessary for the operation
of its smelter. Prices were being bid up to such levels
that M'Zaita found that it could no longer operate profit-
ably. Thereupon, it went to the International Minerals
& Metals Corporation with a proposal that it be released
to the extent of 16oo tons per year of blister copper to
be disposed of as it saw fit. In other words, M'Zaita
wished to sell this copper to Japan. The International
Minerals & Metals Corporation acquiesced in this arrange-
ment since by so doing they were able to assure themselves
of a substantial production which would not otherwise be
available if M'Zaita had to compete with the Japanese for
the purchase of the outside ores referred to above.
Under

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Under the present arrangement, therefore,'M'Zaita
has eliminated competition with the Japanese for the
purchase of these ores by treating for the Japanese the
equivalent of 200 tons of metallic copper per month,
leaving 600 tons of its production for the International
Minerals & Metals Corporation. The latter, incidentally,
sells its copper solely in this country or in England.
If Mr. Elkan's story is correct, his company and
M'Zaita were forced into this arrangement in order to
obtein five-sixths of a loaf at the expense of the other
sixth. Mr. Elkan said that in order to prevent this
copper from going to Japen, leaving aside for the moment
any considerations relating to the contract, it would be
necessary for M'Zaita to pay $30 per ton more for the
ores in question.

My dear Mr. Welles:
AND RECORDS
Department of State
Very many thanks for your letter of July 17th - very greatly appreciated.
I have for a year past been striving to call Mr. Hull's attention to the
folly of continuing to supply gasoline to the Japs wherewith to murder
their neighbors.
I hand you herewith a repitition of the cable sent to our President from
George A. Fitch in Chungking where he is coordinator between the Chinese
Government and the industrial cooperatives to help in the reorganization
of China's industries.
894.24/1564
The United China Relief striving to procure 5 Million Dollars relief funds
for China would seem to be almost useless and money thrown away as long
s we are supplying Japan with the power - that they cannot optain else-
where and do not possess of their own - to continue to operate their
bombers over the civilian population of China.
I have carefully noted all that you wrote me but it does not get us
anywhere in connection with the steps that you refer to. On the other
hand I read in this morning's paper as follows "Japan Reaffirms Axis
Alliance Ties".
Furthermore Hitler has said to have asked Japan to help him under date
of July 18th and as for the new Jap Cabinet I hand you herewith the
pictures of two of the r most important ministers - Foreign and Finance.
7
I am a phrenologist and if there is any human nature benevolence or
0
nobleness indicated in these visages then I don't know my phrenology.
4.24
With great esteem,
Faithfully,

Hon'l. Tom Connerly
U. S. Senator
Chairman Foreign Relations Committee
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir and Senator:
Frankly, are we as a Nation afraid to go to the bat
with Japan?
I hope not and sincerely believe not; BUT, I do
believe that ultimately, likely very soon we are going to
War with Japan, the Nation seems to believe it--then just
why do we sell oIL to them of any kind, or in any form?
Just two(2) big Jap. ship tankers
out of California (145,000 Barrels
of diesel fuel oil)--this week--
thank you.
That at least is AP/ News in our dailies yesterday; are
we still APPEASING these treacherous lieing orientals?
Well we will wake up some fine morning and find that they
have struck us hard--and below the belt, really that as
high as they can hit--ethically or physically.
I think that we had better quit appeasement as it
relates to war, politics, Labor Unions, France and Japan
but take our gloves off and get busy withoutofurther delay
before--"we miss the boat".
Very sincerely,
T. Y. HILL.

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
COPY
BRITISH EMBASBY
WASHINGTON, D.C.,
July 18th, 1941.
SAFE HAND
Dear Finletter,
Recent information which has reached us is to the
effect that the Japanese are making inquiries as to
Bolivian "yungas" product and have been asking for samples
to be sent to Tokyo.
I understand that "yungas" is the name of the forest
region east of La Paz which includes the rubber-growing
area on the Marmore, tributary to the Amazon. The yungas
produot referred to above is undoubtedly rubber of which
there used to be a small export (around about one thousand
tons per annum) 1n the years prior to 1936. The rubber is
exported principally through Para along with Amazon produo-
tion and we have records of certain small quantities which
have been recently exported from that port.
In the normal way it may be assumed that Bolivian
rubber passing in this way to Para would become subject
to Brazilian export restrictions but from the fact that
the Japanese are now making inquiries with regard to this
product it would appear likely that they are proposing
to find some other means of shipping it. Under these cir-
cumstances, while the quantities are not likely to reach
any very alarming figure, you may perhaps like to keep
this point in mind in any further negotiations you may
have with Bolivia for restriotion of their exports to
undesirable destinations.
Yours sincerely,
G. F. THOROLD
Mr. T. K. Finletter,
Special Assistant to the Secretary,
Room 362, U.8. Department of State,
Washington, D. C.

PARnciPAnT: Mr. Walden and Mr. Singer, of Standard Vacuum Company;
Mr. Hiss;
Mr. Hornbeck (toward the end of the conversation).

Mr. Walden for several days past has been asking for the Depart-
mont's views as to whether his Company should apply to the etherlands
East Indies authorities for the recently required export permits for
shipments of petroleum products to Jap'n and also as to whether the
Dopartment will recommend to the Treasury Department that the Japanese
be allowed to transfer dollars to Standard Vaouum in payment for such
SEP.8-
shipments.
Mr. Walden said that in his opinion public criticism of oil ship-
monts to Japan is so great that his Company cannot accept the responsi-
S
/NW
bility for making such sales without the approval of this Government.
 said that in the past he has been willing to deal on an informal
mois but that he thinks he should get some definite approval in writing
at

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
present time--later he indicated that a formal grant of an
hange license to the Japanese interests concerned for the
insfer of dollars in New York might constitute such formal approval.
Both Mr.. Walden and Mr. Singer said that they considered that
this Government's decision with respect to further shipments of
petroleum from the Netherlands East Indies to Japan will be considered
by the Japanese as a "touchstone" of our policy. As Mr. Singer
expressed it, if an assurance is given that the continuance of ship-
ment will be permitted, the Japanese will say "Ah, not so tough ".
On the other hand, they both considered that refusal to permit such
shipments would be a matter of grave importance.
Mr. Walden said that he had been in touch with Mr. Schmidt, of
the Treasury Department, who said that he could give no information
until the whole matter had been cleared with the Department of State.
Mr. Walden said that he will not give his representatives in the
Indies instructions to apply for export permits unless he first
receives formal approval by the United States Government for the
transfer of exchange--partly because his Company does not wish to
accept payment in guilders or in yen; primarily because of the con-
siderations mentioned above. He said that he had told Mr. Schmidt
plainly that the financial aspect of the problem to his Company was
unimportant. His Company wishes to be guided entirely by and to
follow fully the policies of the United States Government.
At

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
At this point Mr. Hornbeck was able to see Mr. Walden for a few
moments and recommended that Mr. Walden postpone any action with
respect to exports from the Indies to Japan for a few days.
Mr. Walden said that he hoped that he could hear not later than
Monday, August 4, as several vessels will be loading in the Indies by
that time.

Mr. Acheson talked first briefly to Mr. Noel Hall and told him that
it had beon decided that the wisest policy for this Government to follow
with respect to advice to the Standard-Vacuum Company regarding that
company's shipments of petroleum from the Netherlands East Indies to
Japan would be to inform the company substantially as follows: (1) As
to the contraot entered into in November 1940 between the Standard-Vacuum
Company and the Japanese Company we would suggest to the Standard-Vaouum
Company that it not make application to the Netherlands East Indies
Government for export licenses for shipments of petroleum oalled for in
A
FILFD
the contract. It would be presumed that the purohasing company, i.6,
PS/
the Japanese Company, would be sufficiently interested to make suoh
19
applioations if it desired to continue to receive shipments of petrdloum
under the contract. The Japanese Company appears in any event to be the
appropriate

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
riate party to make such application; (2) With respect to the
any's normal business with Japan carried on by its own branoh in
under a quota established by the Japanese Government, it would
be wise to make no decision at the present time and the company should
make no application for the present for export licenses.
Mr. Noel Hall said that he considered the proposal wise and that
his authorization was sufficiently broad to permit him to say without
hesitation and without referring the matter to his Government that the
British Government would adopt a like policy with respect to the
Shell Company.
At this point in the conversetion Dr. Loudon and Baron van
Boetzelaer were received by Mr. Acheson, Mr. Hall remeining with the
oonferees.
Mr. Acheson thereupon explained to the Netherlands representatives
the above proposal of this Government stating that the British Govern-
ment was in accord with our position. Mr. Acheson went on to explain
that we felt that the Netherlands Indies Government should be in position
to determine with complete freedom of action what, if any, part of the
companies' shipments of petroleum should continue to go forward to Japan
under present conditions. It was explained that in order to make it
clear that this Government was not recommending that the Netherlands
Government decide to continue existing shipments but was leaving the
matter up to the Indies Government, this Government had requested the
American Company--the British Government is taking similar action with
respect

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
ot to the Shell Company--to make no applications and no representa
0
ons in the matter. Mr. Acheson further stated that in order to accord
y decision of the Netherlands Government full support he wished to say
that in the event that the Netherlands Government decided to issue export
libenses for any petroleum products belonging to the two companies, the
United States Government would authorize payment from blocked Japanese
(2)
funds in the United States to the companies concerned and, on the other
hand, in the event that the Netherlands Government decided to refuse
'any export licenses for petroleum products, this Government correspond-
ingly would refuse to issue licenses under our freezing orders for pay-
ment for such shipments and, further, would be glad to announce this
publicly in order to show that ve were in full agreement with the
Netherlands Government and that, in fact, it would be useless in any
such case for the Netherlands Government to authorize export licenses
since by virtue of the policy of the United States Government payment
could not be made for the authorized shipments.
Dr. Loudon and Baron van Boetzelaer listened attentively and asked
a number of questions. They expressed their appreciation for the con-
sideration shown to the Netherlands Indies Government and said that they
would obtain the reaction of that Government to the proposals ir. Acheson
had outlined. Mr. Acheson made it clear that with respect to the normal
or quota business our tentative decision to hold the matter in abeyance
would remain in effect for about a week, at the end of which time the
matter could be reconsidered, we hoped with the benefit of the views of
the Netherlands Indies Government.
Dr. Loudon

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.98)
Loudon remarked that as was obvious,political as well as eco-
o considerations were involved. He said that the Indies Government
ght wish to discuss the relevant political questions with the United
tates Government before arriving at a decision. Mr. Acheson replied
that he understood this perfectly and that if it developed that the
Indies Government did desire to raise political questions, Dr. Loudon
should take such questions up with the Secretary of State.
At this point Dr. Loudon had to leave the meeting in order to. keep
an appointment with the Under Secretary. Mr. Acheson then made avail-
able in strict confidence to Mr. Hall and to Baron van Boetzelaer
copies of a memorandum prepared in Co listing the detailed restrictions
now in effect with respect to the export of petroleum products from
the United States to Japan. This memorandum indicated which items are
on a completely prohibited basis and es to the other items set forth
the quantities which will be permitted to be exported during the re-
maining half of this yeer. Mr. Acheson orally described, again in
strict confidence, the other restrictions vis-à-vis Japan with respect
to exports and imports. Both Mr. Hall and Baron van Boetzelaer expressed
their appreciation for the information which had been supplied to them
and said that it would be helpful to their governments.
Previous to the above meeting Mr. Acheson had informed Mr. Walden,
Chairman of the Board of the Standard-Vacuum Company, of the Department's
deoision. Mr. Walden had expressed himself as completely cooperative
in spirit and had said that he would impress upon Mr. Wilkinson of the
Bholl Company the importance of the Shell Company's following the same
srooedure.

1-1408
Mr. Parker and Mr. May, having made an appointment by
894.24/1569
long-distance telephone, called at the Department in regard
to the difficult position in which the North China mangger
for the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company, Mr. F. J. Twogood,
Shanghai, found himself as a result of Japanese pressure.
The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company, which has recently been
permitted by the Japanese military to make shipments of
petroleum products from Shanghai to points along the
AIL
FILHD
Yangtze, has been informed by the Japanese military that
8
if the company refuses to supply considerable quantities
of petroleum products, including 86 octane gasoline, the
facilities recently granted the company would be cut off
and severe restrictions upon its activities imposed. The
company

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
company was given to understand that its decision in the
matter would be considered as an indication of the company's
policy and that the attitude of the Japanese forces toward
the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company would be influenced ac-
cordingly. Mr. Parker indicated that the North China
manager for the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company was "on the
spot". He said that the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company had
avoid
n
been able to
 the demand for the supply of gasoline
from the Dutch East Indies by simply saying that no gaso-
line was available. He said, however, that this excuse
was not available in so far as gasoline from the West Coast
of the United States was concerned because the Japanese
were prepared to provide a tanker to transport the gaso-
line from the West Coast to Shanghai. He said that if
the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company stated that it could not
obtain an export permit for 86 octane gasoline and some
other oil company with no investment in China were to
supply the gasoline, the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company would
be placed in an untenable position vis-à-vis the Japanese.
He said that the Japanese would have to have storage
facilities in China for the petroleum products and he very
much feared that the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company would be
faced with the commandeering of Standard-Vaouum Oil Company
storage facilities in China for the storage of petroleum
products

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
roduots purchased from other oil companies by the
Japanese. Mr. Parker said that he felt that it was un-
fair for his company to have to bear the burden of
refusing to sell gasoline to the Japanese and he hoped
that the Government could assume that responsibility by
rerusing to grant export licenses.
Mr. Parker and Mr. May were informed that, although
they could not use such information vis-a-vis the Japanese,
no licenses had in fact been issued for some months for the
exportation to Japan or occupied China of gasoline of an
86 octane rating. This information seemed good news to
Mr. Parker and Mr. May. They asked, however, whether
unused outstanding licenses issued several months ago
would be revoked. They were told that they could be
given no assurance in this respect.
Mr. Parker was asked whether his company had con-
sidered temporizing in the matter. He replied that the
problem under discussion was already some two months old.
Mr. Parker handed Mr. Hamilton a copy of the telegram
dated July 16 which he had received from the Shanghai
manager of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company outlining the
situation and Mr. Parker drafted a suggested reply to that
message. (Both are attached hereto, together with a copy
of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company's message of July 15 to
1ts

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
ita manager at Shanghai.)
Mr. Parker said that if the company were to quote the
Japanese on 86 octane gasoline in the expectation that it
would not be able to get action upon its applicationXor an
export permit, the company would be placed in an embarrassing
position if permission were forthcoming.
Mr. Parker and Mr. May were told that the matter would
be reported to the higher authorities and that Mr. Adams
would expect to telephone to Mr. Parker or to Mr. May in
regard to the matter on July 18.
(Note: The attitude of Mr. Parker and Mr. May in-
dioated that the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company's extreme
reluctance to supply 86 octane gasoline to the Japanese
armed forces was due to their concern about public opinion
both in China and the United States.)
(Note: During the conversation Messrs. Parker and
May first proposed that the Department authorize them to
reply that the American Government would not permit ex-
portation of the gasoline in question to occupied China.
Mr. Hamilton and Mr. Adams both commented that they did
not think that the Department would wish to authorize
the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company to make such a statement.)

During the course of a conversation with Mr.
15
Donnelly I referred to three different shipping matters;
1) Bunkering of Japanese vessels - I referred to
the Department's recent telegram indiceting that the
United States policy was to give such bunker facilities
to Japan only in the minimum amount necessary for the
Japanese vessels to make their ports. Mr. Donnelly stated
that the Brazilians were adopting a similar policy. I
took occasion to mention that of course any coal and
petroleum furnished to Japanese vessels was a tapping of
Brazilian supplies in the face of a very difficult
supply situation. I repeated that it was the Departmentle
40
view that all such supplies should be devoted primarily
to Inter American commerce and Western Hemisphere defenee
2)

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
2) Immobilized ships - I referred to the Depart-
ment's telegram of Thursday regarding the immobilized ships
and repeated that the Department was very anxious that
Brazil accept the plan. With respect to the question
of the German vessels and our views on allowing the
NORDNERNEY to depart, I stated that beyond the fact that
we could not obtain safe passage from the British Govern-
ment, the Department felt that it could not comment in such
a matter, which was obviously up to the decision of
the Brazilians. I pointed out that, of course, this
Government hoped that Brazil would very shortly put into
service as many vessels as possible. Mr. Donnelly stated
that they would report very promptly on the status of the
matter.
3) British ship warrants - I referred to Rio's
832.85/197,196
telegrams nos. 1030 end 1038 and stated that the Depart-
ment was going into the matter informally with the British.
I stated that the Department would inform the Embassy in
more detail but that our general view was that shipping
within the Western Hemisphere, and especially coastwise
shipping, was a matter of concern to the American republics
and that the British should not attempt to. control it.

Mr. Harold Ickes,
Washington, D C.
Dear Mr. Ickes:
By the papers I am informed that we are about to be de-rived
of our one method of keeping cool on Sunday afternoon, namely we are
to be asked to reduce our use of gasoline unless it is absolurely neo-
essary.
Is shis so that we can sell more of it to Japan for the
purpose not only of fightly our ally,wChina, but as I understand it
they have reserves for one year WITH WHICH THEY CAN FIGHT US.
That is the craziest thing I have ever heard of. Is it
so that the Standard Oil and other big oil companies will not lose their
revenus. Are we to be kioked around by Japan's foilbes? Let them BUY
their gas from "he Dutch Indies, if they try to get it otherwise then
lets step in and give them what Germany says she must get. If Japan has
no more sense that to let the carpet chewer tell her what to do let her
take the consequences.
I for one dont feel called upon to save on gas when it is
being shipped to Japan with whom we may soon be in war.
we have been sending the serap iron for the manufacture of
schrapnel to kill Chinese ever since the Sino-Jap war started and we
say that China is our friend and ally. Such assininty is beyond my
ken. Oh, yes, I know there are plenty of exouses but they are devoid of
reason. Let Japan keep her silk, we can use rayon or calico, China
perfers Calico to silk anyway, because she has plenty of sålk and calioo
is IMPORTED. Slush.
2012AUG20'41
Yours,
PP.Ppfledn.

The Honorable

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's instruc-
1565A
tion No. 225 of July 25, 1941, transmitting a copy of a
letter from the First Secretary of the British Embassy in
Washington with reference to the possibility that thesJa-
anese may endeavor to ship to Japan a product of the yungas
PS/MS
region of Bolivia.
According

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
According to the local British Legation the above in-
formation was forwarded from the Buenos Aires mission, which
in turn received it by cable from the Foreign Office in
London. NABEI, Buenos Aires agent for Mitsui, was sup-
posed to have been requested, from Santiago, to book space
on the "Buenos Aires Maru" at Buenos Aires for 40 tons of
Para rubber. Previous communications between the same parties
indicated that one Proceso KUTO, who operates in Santiago
and La Paz, is the buying agent for such material and that
he has been authorized to buy Para rubber at a price up to
26 1/2 cents. He was also asked how much "Yungas" product
he could supply monthly and vwas instructed to send samples
of both items to Buenos Aires and Tokyo.
Neither the British nor this Legation has been able
thus far to learn any more details concerning the matter.
So far as can be determined, Proceso Kuto is not known in
La Paz nor has anything been heard with reference to Jap-
anese attempts to purchase rubber in Bolivia. The Legation
will continue its efforts, however, and should anything
further be learned the Department will be advised accord-
ingly.
Respectfully yours,
Douglas Jenkins

Dear Acheson:
You were kind enough yesterday to think that my suggestion that
some of your officers and ours might examine the problem of oil supplies
for Japan was worth further consideration with a view to action. My
idea was that perhaps two, or at the most three aside might be asked to
examine:
1. Estimates of Japan's stocks and requirements of oil
2. The effect on her current supplies of the withdrawal of non-
894.24
Japanese tankers
894.24/.
3. The possible implications of ships warrants and bunker control
in reducing by means of indirect pressure supplies of petroleum
products reaching Japan.
You referred to Mr. Thornburg, and by a coincidence I met him at
dinner the night after our talk. He lunched with me today to meet Mr.
Wyndham-White, who knows a good deal about policy with regard to tankers
5
I told him of our conversation.
00
I am very anxious that we should keep our administrative arrangements
closely in step with one another, and if you agree to go forward wth
ILED
the suggestion I have made, we will be happy to collaborate in any
c3
practical way.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON

August 27, 1941
The Department has been informed
by a telegram from the Embassy in Mexico
that Japan cancelled export licenses for
Bilk and rayon destined for a Mexican
port becauee the Mexican Government 1s
refusing to allow the exportation of
strategic minerals to Japan.
An effort is being made to secure
a rayon supply for Mexico from the
United States. The Embassy in Colombia
has reported that Japan has refused to
sell silk to Colombia unless Colombia
agrees to sell platinum to Japan.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
My dear Finletter
Thank you for your leter of August
19th regarding shipments of Manganese to Japan.
894.24/1586
The position, in what you may term
the pre-crisis period, was that exports of Manganese to
Japan from India were restricted to 10,000 tons a month,
which was approximately 80% of normal trade, and from
Malaya to 1,700 tons a month, being about 60% of normal
trade. The first stage in further restrictions was to
reduce these quotas by 50%, and this no doubt accounts
for the figure of 5,000 tons per month which you quote
in your letter as having been the figure for exports
from India.
Now, however, a further step has
been taken under which no Export Licenses will be granted
for any commodities from the Empire to Japan except in
special cases where funds have become available as a re-
sult of permitted imports from Japan of commodities of
particular value for war purposes. Even so, licenses
will only be granted for goods of least essential value
to Japan. As a result of this we understand that no
licenses for Manganese are now being granted for ship-
ments to Japan, and if, in fact, 1,000 tons has been
exported, as quoted in your letter, I think it may be
taken that this had been in respect of a contract already
SEP 5 1941
made and paid for before the freezing regulations were
brought in.
As far as Malaya is concerned, there
are some added difficulties, in view of the fact that the
PS/MM
mines
Mr. T. K. Finletter,
Special Assistant to the Secretary,
United States Department of State,
Washington, D.C.
GFT:MS

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.97)
Mr. T. K. Finletter
-2-
August 21st, 1941
mines are Japanese-owned and the position is somewhat the
same as in the case of exports of iron ore from the Phil-
ippines. It is the intention to reduce these Manganese
exports from Malaya as far as possible, but we have not
yet received information as to the exact position at the
moment.
I hope to be able to give you more precise
details in both these cases before long but, in the mean-
time, you may take it little or no Manganese is at present
being exported from India to Japan.

A SURVEY OF THIS GOVERNMENT'S POLICY

OIVISION 
In recent years, the question of what course of
ments of oil from the United States to Japen has been a
complex issue compounded of many elements. This Depart-
894.24/1587
ment in determining the course of action which it would
follow has taken into account the developing international
situations in the Far East and in Europe, the advice of
our military and naval authorities, the state of publio
opinion in this country and considerations of an economio
nature. In what follows an attempt has been made to trace
the main developments and factors whioh have contributed
711.
to the forming of the policy adopted by this Department.
1587
The decisions which this Department has taken upon
the couree of action to be followed with regard to ship-

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.99)

background of the Far Eastern situation as a whole. In a
brief space. only some of the significant features of that
situation as it developed may be mentioned.
The current phase of the Far Eastern situation began,
from many points of view, with Japan's military occupation
of Manchuria in 1931. There followed in 1933 the Japanese
occupation of Jehol. In April 1934 the so-called "Amau
statement" was issued which made clear that Japan asserted
with respect to China a special and exclusive relation. In
December 1934 Japan gave notice of a desire to terminate the
Washington Naval Treaty thus indicating an intention to resume
competitive naval construction in the Pacific. In 1935 the
Steps in
"autonomy movement" south of the Great Wall
Japan s ex-
pansionist
in China was developed with evident Japanese
policy,
1931-1937
participation. In 1936 Germany and Japan con-
oluded the anti-Comintern pact to which Italy became a party
in 1937. In July 1937 the current hostilities between China
and Japan commenced.
This Government was the first of the great powers to
beaome alive to the full implications of Japan's course of
This Govern-
action not only for the peoples of the
ment's attitude
with regard to
Pacifio but also for the continued existence
Japan's program,
1931-1937
of order among nations based upon law. In
publio statements this Government clearly indicated the results
which

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
which might be expected to follow from a substitution of
force for law as the basis of international relationships
and it set forth the principles which it regarded as essen-
tial to normal and mutually satisfactory relations among
nations. This Government also made evident its disapproba-
tion of the course of action being followed by Japan and
declined to recognize the regime which was set up in Manchuria
under Japanese auspices. At the same time a willingness was
expressed to assist in working out a solution of existing
problems in the Pacific by peaceful processes. This Govern-
ment's attitude was clearly shown, for example, in the
messages exchanged between the Japanese Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Mr. Hirota, and the Secretary on February 21 and
March 3, 1934; in the statement to the Japanese Government
of April 29, 1934 with regard to the so-called "Amau state-
ment" referred to above; and in the statement by the Secre-
tary on December 6, 1935, in connection with the "autonomy
movement" in north China.
With the commencement of the current hostilities in
China in 1937, this Government continued its efforts to
Commencement
persuade Japan that its best interests
of hostilities
1n 1937

In determining what action could and should be taken by

The immediate reaction of the American people to the
commencement of hostilities in China in l937 was a feeling

this Government followed a middle course of action. It

Despite criticism from many quarters, this Government
refrained from applying the Neutrality Act in the hostilities
between China and Japan because of the conviction that
an application of that Act would adversely affect China's
position. This Government consistently declined to
give its assent to situations brought about by Japanese
authorities or agents in China in violation of treaties
and international law and through the unwarranted use
of force. By a reasoned approach and through diplomatic
means this Government sought with patience and persistence
to protect American rights and interests in the Far East,

CONCLUSIONS
The decisions taken in this Department upon the course
of action to be followed with respect to oil shipments to
Japan have been related necessarily to developments in
the Far Eastern situation and to this Government's general
894.24
polioy with regard to that situation. Among the factors
determining the Department's decisions upon the question
of oil shipments to Japan have been the state of public
15
opinion in this country, which on the whole has ardently
desired non-involvement by this country in any hostilities
and whioh was slow in overcoming an aversion to measures in
the nature of sanotions; and the unwillingness of other
oil producing countries to cooperate in an oil embargo
against Japan unless this Government would give a guaran-
tee of military assistance in case difficulties with Japan
should result. With the outbreak of the European war
the question of oil shipments to Japan became only one
factor in the general strategic situation. Our military
GRET
and naval authorities advised that this Government was
not

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
-26
not prepared fer war on the Atlantio and the Pacific
simultaneously and that action likely to precipitate
trouble in the Far East should therefore be avoided. The
gaining of time to prepare became a paramount considera-
tion which affected this Government's policy toward oil
shipments, not because it was felt that an embargo upon
oil shipments would lead to an attack by Japan upon the
United States, but because an embargo would tend to lead
Japan to use duress or military force against British or
Netherland possessions in the Far East. It has been felt
that such a result would have been prejudicial to the
cause to which we were committed and to the progress of
this country's rearmament program, and might result in
war between the United States and Japan.
Additional considerations weighing against the cutting
off of all oil supplies to Japan have been the desire not
to impel Japan, or to give Japan an excuse, to move against
the Netherlands Indies while they were helpless after the
occupation of the Netherlands by Germany, and the desire
not to prejudice the ability of the Netherlands Indies to
hold their own in the discussions relating to the oil
shipments sought by a Japanese economic delegation in the
Netherlands Indies. This Government also wished to give
SECRET
an opportunity

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.99)
-27
opportunity for the full consideration of a proposal
put forward by the Japanese Ambassador here with a view to
bringing peace in the Pacific area.
Although all oil shipments to Japan from the United
States were not cut off, shipments of "aviation" gasoline
were stopped in the interests of national defense and
shipments of other petroleum products were permitted only un-
der license.
During the two years of time which have thus far been
gained, this Department has urged that the defenses of the
United States in the Pacific be increased and that the
British and Netherland Governments be enoouraged to in-
crease their Far Eastern defenses. The strategic posi-
tion of the United States with regard to the Pacific has
been strengthened month by month through the progress of
its rearmament and naval expansion programs; through the
building up of the defenses of the Philippines, Guam and
Samoa; and through the reinforcement with American en-
couragement and assistance of the defenses of Singapore
and other British possessions in the Far East and of the
Netherlands East Indies. This Government, moreover, has
extended material, financial, technical and moral as-
sistance to the Chinese Government; in recent months
this Government's determination to assist China has been
emphasized through the extension of aid under the "Lend-
Lease" Act. With the conclusion of the Three Power
Paot,

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.97)
-28
moreever, opinion in this country hardened with
regard to Japan so that support could be found for measure
such as, ultimately, the freezing of Japanese assets in
this country. Meantime although Japan has been receiving sub
samtial shipments of certain types of oil from the United
States, Japan's economic position and Japanese morale have
been drained by two more years of hostilities in China.
Japan's position has also been adversely affected by the
outbreak of hostilities between Germany and the Soviet
Union, which has confronted Japan with the prospect that
if hostilities should develop in the Pacific as a con-
aequence of Japanese acts, Japan would be open to attack
from all sides, and which has made Japan less likely for
the time being to react to the imposition of an oil embargo
by promptly moving against the Netherlands East Indies.

July 30, 1941
Hon. Elbert D. Thomas,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator Thomas:
From numerous letters, broadcasts, and public statements
issued by you in the past several years, I know that you
have long been a strong advocate of the so-called "appease-
ment" policy on the part of this country toward Japan.
Your pro-Japanese sympathies have been manifest on many
occasions, and are well khown by all of your constituents
who follow the trend of international affairs with any
degree of concem.
I was present at a public forum meeting in Ogden,
Utah, in February, 1939, featuring the well knomn lecturer
Upton Close. At this meeting a letter from you was read
wherein you defended your stand for appeasement of the
Japanese militarists on the grounds that the growing crises
894.24
in the Far East might blow over and there was noneed of
antagonizing the Japanese unnecessarily.
Your constituents feel that the influence you have
exerted in your position in the Senate has in a large part
been responsible for the continued shipments of oil,
scrap-iron, and munitions from this country to Japan. We
are also convinced that had it not been for these shipments
of essential products to the Japanese, these pagan aggressors
would long since have been totally defeated by the valiant
Chinese patriots who are so bravely defending their homes
and lives.
We are curous to know the reason why you and the other
"statesmen" who determine our country's foreign policy have
deemed it wise to continue to supply Japan with the tools of
war which enabled her to wage ruthless and aggressive war
against the Chinese.
Even after Japan allied herself with those blood-
thirsty bastards Hitler and Mussolini in their despicable
plot to destroy all democracies (including our own), we
continued to ship oil, iron, and other essential war
materials for the use of the Japanese army. Why was this?
Even

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Even after this country launched its own defense program,
and the Administration was urging people to conserve gasoline,
to donate kitchen utensils, and to submit to higher taxes,
and to bend every possible effort to the building up of the
United States' defense -- even after this -- we continued
to supply our prospective enemy, Japan, with oil and iron.
Why was this?
I am sure that this Administration has had ample power
to order the cessation of such shipments, had it cared to do
so. Our president is empowered to enforce an embargo of
war materials against any belligerent country, is he not?
It therefore follows that the policy of appeasement
and collaboration with the Japanese has, for reasons known
only to the esoteric, been determined and pursued
deliberately and circumspectly by our national policy-
makers, such as yourself.
Mr. Thomas, I am not asking you to disclose any secrets
of international policy. But I would greatly appreciate a
letter from you stating clearly and concisely just why this
country has been so generous in arming and supplying Japan,
a nation which is opposed to us politically, economically,
and ideologically, and seems about to become our adversary
in a military sense, also.
Very truly yours,
PARKE S. PETTERSON

MEXICAN UNION REFUSES JAPANESE OFFEN OF SILK
Deelinss to Ast When Tokye Sesks Addisional
Shipments of War Supplies--Looks to U. S.
(Cepyright by N. Y. Tribune, Ino.)
Mexieo City, Aug. 18--Japanese importers, it was
learmed today, in an attempt to persaade Nexico to resume
shipments of war supplies to Japan, have offered in ex-
shange to supply Mexieo with all its silk and rayon meeds,
depleted by American defense priority measures.
However, the Textile Workers' Union, to whieh the
offer was made in the expeotation that it would bring
pressure to bear on the Government, has turned it down
because it considers Mexioo's pledge to cooperate in
continental defense more important than the personal
situation of Mexican textile workers.
The union petitioned the Minister of Industries to
begin negotiations with Washington in an attempt to
persuade the United States to lst Mexioo have sufficient
silk and rayen to prevent unemployment of 7,000 workers
in the industry.

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
September 11, 1941
BY SPECIAL MESSENGER
MMORANDUM to Mr. Williemson:
On June 18, 194l, you sent to Mr. Wiley a copy of a letter
from the vice-president of the United States Smelting Refining
894.24/1596-1/2
and Mining Company relative to the attempt made in Mexico by
Robert H. Sherman of Bernard Bandler & Sons, San Francisco, to
secure a considerable quantity of bortz for shipment to Japan.
For your information we have just been informed through
a reliable source that Bernard Bandler & Sons handle commercial
diamonds as a business; that they have a foreign agent in Japan
to whom shipments from this country of carbon only were made
up to about four years ago when they started to ship bortz and
carbon from Brazil to the Japanese agent; that due to diffi-
culties encountered in Brazil they thought bortz might be
shipped from Mexico to Japan and, accordingly, commissioned
Robert H. Sherman to ascertain whether commercial diamonds
could be purchased in Mexico and what business concern could
handle the shipments, but that the efforts of Sherman while
in Mexico were not successful.
It is also reported that the president of the firm repre-
sented that commercial diamonds would not be sold to any firm
in this country unless it were known that the ultimate desti-
nation was an American firm by which the diamonds would be
used in the United States, and that his firm has a gentleman's
agreement as the result of which all sales of this class of
merchandise are invariably reported to the British Board of
Trade.
Sauncul (Cau)
DEC 30 1943

SAMURL KLAUS

Department of Sta
STATE
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
With reference to the Legation's confidential des-
patch No. 978 of July 31, 1941, in which was reported
a Japanese threat to cut off exports of textiles to
Holivia unless this country continued to ship minerals
in return, I have the honor to furnish certain details
regarding further efforts of Japan to obtain such
OCT 3 1941
minerals and to make payment for them.
FIL
Following
(. (

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Following the threat mentioned above, which re-
ceived little attention from the Bolivian Government,
no additional moves were made, openly at least, until
some two weeks ago when the local Japanese Chargé
visited the Minister of National Economy with the
suggestion that Bolivia sell Japan that mineral pro-
duction not already contracted for by other countries
and accept in payment bolivianos, of which Japanese
interests have sizable deposits in the Banco Central.
The Minister showed little interest in this offer and
the Chargé then countered by suggesting a barter deal,
Bolivia to ship minerals and to receive in exchange
Japanese textiles, pottery and other products. This
suggestion was also rather coolly received by the
Minister, who fortunately is very friendly to the
cause of the democracies, and the Legation understands
that the conversation ended on this basis.
The next attempt to arrive at a satisfactory arrange-
ment was made by the Yokohama Specie Bank of Rio de
Janeiro, which requested the Banco Central to open a
dollar account in its name and to accept in exchange
for these dollar credits the bolivianos which the Specie
Bank now has on deposit with the Banco Central. This
request was refused by the Foreign Exchange Commission
(consisting of the Minister of National Economy, and
the managers of the Banco Meroantil, the Banco Nacional,
and the Banco Central) and the Yokohama Bank then suggested
that the Banco Central turn over to the Banco Minero
sufficient

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
sufficient dollars to cover the mineral purchases
which Japanese interests desire to make, accepting in
exchange for these dollars the bolivianos on deposit
with the Banco Central. As observed in seweral pre-
vious despatches, the manager of the Banco Central
sometimes appears to be a little more anxious to
gain a profit than complete friendliness to the anti-
totalitarian struggle wauld justify and he was, accord-
ing to the Legation's information, disposed to accede
to this last request, in spite of the fact that the
result for all practical purposes would have been ex-
actly the same as though the Specie Bank had been per-
mitted to open a dollar account. He is understood
to have been overruled by the other members of the
Exchange Commission, however, and the Japanese are
consequently casting about for otner possibilities.
One of these possibilities which has come to the
attention of the Legation, and one which is believed
to have been effectively, though unofficially, blocked,
was a proposal to a large Bolivian commercial firm
(Jorge Saenz e Hijos) by a local Japanese concern
owned by one Armando H. ONO that SAENZ act as inter-
mediary for the house of Yamamoto, Kobe, in a barter
deal such as that suggested previously to the Minister
of National Economy. The details of the proposed trans-
action were as follows: Saenz e Hijos would purchaae
for dollars antimony, lead, copper and quinine which
they would ship to Yamamoto and for which they would
receive in exchange Japanese goods to be sold locally.
In

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
In order to carry on transactions of the kind
described for any length of time Yamamoto would have
to reach an agreement with a very well-established
and financially sound Bolivian concern, since the
arrangement would involve continual dollar expendi-
tures and boliviano reciepts. Unless the Bolivian
firm were at the same time carrying on other business
transactions to replenish its supply of dollars 
the entire arrangement would fall of its own weight
after the first exchange. It is believed that Saenz
is one of the few Bolivian concerns which could possibly
do this and that if, as is believed almost certain,
Saenz refuses the proposal the Japanese will experience
considerable difficulty in locating another organization
capable of even considering such an arrangement.
The Legation understands that no shipments of
Japanese goods have been made to Bolivia for the past
six weeks, so it appears that Japan is following
through on its threat to cut off exports to Bolivia.
However, this is easy to understand because, unless
Japan is able to obtain Bolivian minerals in exchange,
it will be impossible for that country to receive
payment for any shipments it might make. Thus far the
effect of this cutting off of Japanese exports has not
been felt, since Japanese retail stores in Bolivia
are well-stecked and there are also understood to be
large quantities of merchandise in Arica awaiting
shipment to La Paz. What the reaction will be if
these stocks are exhausted and higher prices have
to

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
to be paid for similar products imported from England
or the United States (or  if. they cannot be bought
at any price) cannot be foreseen, but there seems as
yet to be little or no concern in official circles
over the situation.
Attempts are still being made by Bolivia to obtain
an enlarged quota, amounting to £400,000, for impor-
tations from England (see despatch No. 978 of July 31),
and if this is granted the problem would be partially
solwed. However, in view of the fact that total im-
portations of cotton textiles in to Bolivia during
1940 amounted to approximately £400,000, of which
some £3009000 came from Japan and only £19,000 from
England, it seems probable that some difficulty may
be experienced. As a matter of fact Bolivia's impor-
tations of these products from the United States
during 1940, about £25,000, were greater than from
England, and our country may be in a better position
at this time to assume the extra business than England.
In the meantime, payments for those Bolivian
minerals which the Japanese have been able to obtain
are being made by many devious means. In this con-
nection reference is made to the Lima Embassy's despatch
No. 1638 dated August 28, 1941, a copy of which was
forwarged to this Legation, and the following additional
examples of such payments are offered for the Depart-
ment's information. These data were obtained from an
employee of the Banco Minero who is believed to be
reliable.
Before

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
Bofore. August, 1941:
The Banco Italiano, Lima, deposited to the oredit
of the Banco Minero, La Paz, in the National City Bank,
Lima, drafts against the National City Bank, New York.
These deposits were made by the Banco Italiano for
Cia. Peruana de Comercio Intercontinental S. A. Ltda.,
Lima, who in turn was acting for Bernardo Elsner
(Casa Bernardo).
The Bank of America, San Francisco, made deposits
to the credit of the Banco Minero in the National
City Bank, New York, for Sanyei Yoko (Kyllmann Bauer
& Cia.).
Before and after August, 1941:
Cia. Comercial Taibo S. A. (Santiago, Chile)
deposits drafts on New York Banks in the Banco de
Chile, Santiago, to the credit of the Banco Minero.
Mitsubishi S. A. Chile; same as above.
Cia. Comercial Taibo S. A. and Mitsubishi S. A.,
Chile, through the Banco de Chile advise the Banco
Central of Bolivia to draw against the Chilean bank's
account in the Chemical Bank & Trust Co., New York,
to the favor of Banco Minero, which in turn deposits
the drafts in the Grace National Bank, New York.
C. Itoh & Co., Sanwa & Co., Mitsui & Co. Ltd.,
Matsura Trading Co., whose local agents are res-
pectiwely Lucio Nishi, T. Komori & Co., H. Harjes,
and Ochiay & Co., carry out the same financial
transaction as that described above, except that
they work through the Banco do Brasil.
Respectfully yours,
Doccaa Jium

Dear Finletter,
894.202/2/536 In a memorandum attached to my letter to
you of July 3rd under the heading "Petleonsa" there is a re-
port of efforts being made by Mitsubishi, Tokyo, to import
well drilling machinery via their Mexican branch.
London now inform us that they have learned
of similar efforts being made in Buenos Aires. The report
is to the effect that Mitsubishi, Buenos Aires, have obtained
estimates for four well drilling machines from the National
894.24/1602
Supply Company. Mitsubishi, Buenos Aires, transmitted par-
ticulars to their Ore Department in Tokyo on July 1Oth, and
it therefore appears more than probable that efforts are
being made to obtain the machinery ostensibly for the Argen-
tine, with the intention of subsequently reexporting it to
Japan.
The following details, which are somewhat
sketchy, may help to identify the orders:
1)
The cost of each machine was quoted at $62,235
f.a.s. New York, or $70,326 f.o.b. Buenos Aires.
Delivery in Buenos Aires five months after
placing the order.
2)
The following details of the order are incomplete
but I am giving them to you in case they are of

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.99)
Townsend, Ga.
August 27, 1941.
Mr. Thomas H. Beck,
Editorial Director,
Collier's,
250 Park Avenue,
New York City.
Dear Sir:
I have just read the August 23rd Collier's Editori-
al entitled "Cooperation American Plan."
The next to the last paragraph in this article reads
as follows:- Don't be upset by the fact that the Japanese
have been allowed to buy some American petroleum. The
Japanese provided their own tankers. They took nothing
we needed. We got what we wanted in return.
2
Yes, the Japanese did provide their own tankers and
American boats also carried oil to Japan. A friend of
mine just returned from Japan the latter part of July on
the ship "James Lykes" of the Lykes Brothers Steamship
Company of New Orleans, La., and their cargo was oil.
He said other American boats also were carrying oil to
Japan. Too, he stated that the "James Lykes" was not a
tanker and the oil was shipped in drums or barrels.
On the return trip, their cargo was sugar and copra
picked up on two stops in the Phillipines. The return
cargo being what it was I can't understand the last sen-
tence in your paragraph above quoted. I say this for the
reason that there might be merchandise a tanker could
not bring back to us from Japan but surely a cargo freighter
could and yet it brought Phillipine merchandise.
I think you should correct this paragraph as it does
not present the whole truth and I know Collier's would
not have it otherwise.
Very truly yours,
JAMES M. DeFoor

 In Mr. Hall's absence I am writing to give you
upon our policy with regard to China.

It has been decided that to prevent the Japanese
benefiting from our exports to occupied China, it is
894.24/1605
necessary to control by licensing all exports to China
except those going via the Burma Road. To avoid injury
to legitimate Chinese interests pending the making of
detailed arrangements, it is intended to grant licenses
freely within the limits of normal trade for exports other
than -
(a) those not available for supply reasons
894.
(b) those contained in the annexed list which
has been called the "Nil List"
whit of cotion?
(c) any specific consignments where there are
strong reasons for refusing on grounds of
economic warfare.
Action on the above lines is being taken forthwith
by the United Kingdom and the Colonies. India, Burma and
the Dominions are being invited to take similar action.
The Government of Hong Kong, however, is being given some
PS/MW
latitude in the application of the abovementioned policy.

u

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
It is intended to tighten up the procedure mentioned
abovg without injury to friendly interests. It is pro-
poses to collect as much information as possible about
the trustworthiness of the individual consignees so that
exports may be confined to those of which, so far as may
be judged, the potential advantages to free China are
likely to outweigh the danger of leakage to Japan. In
particular, it is intended to take advantage of the
advice of friendly banks in Shanghai who are being asked
to avoid (where possible in conformity with the polioy of
U.S. banks) any financial transactions likely to benefit.
Japan. In the interim period until the necessary admin-
istration machinery can be organized, we propose that
Empire Governments will issue licenses without reference
in cases where the danger of leakage to Japan does not
seem to be over-riding and where delay must be avoided in
order to prevent trade being brought to a standstill.
In addition to the consignee control on the lines
mentioned above, it is suggested that it may be desirable
to super-impose quantitative control of exports of par-
ticular commodities. Trade in such goods is to be
separately examined in the light of the quantities generally
required by reliable consignees, and their value to Japan
if they were seized.
The arrangements mentioned in this letter do not apply
wett?
to petroleum products.

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
DCR
Dear Mr. Acheson,
In Mr. Hall's absence, I am writing to inform
894.24/1606
you that it has been decided in London that control
of exports from the British Empire to Japan will be
exercised by export licensing machinery and not by
financial machinery under the Freezing Order. If an
Export License is granted for a transaction, the
necessary financial licenses for the payments involved
will be granted more or less automatically. In
practice, trade will be confined to specially approved
transactions.
I am sending a copy of this letter to Mr. Cochran
1606
for his information.
894.
Yours sincerely,
R. J. STOPFORD

In Mr. Hall's absence, I am writing to inform
you that it has been decided in Loncon that control of
exports from the British Empire to Japan will be exercised
by export licensing machinery and not by financial
machinery under the Freezing Order. If an Export Licanse
is granted for a transaction, the necessary financial
894.24/1606
licenses for the payments involved will be ranted more
or less automatically. In practice, trade will be
confined to specially approved transactions.

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 21, 1941
Under present procedure, the Department's
air mail instructions to Tokyo go via clipper
to Hong Kong, thence by open mail to Tokyo.
Arrangements for forwarding of commercial air
mail from Hong Kong are entirely up to the
postal authorities there.
Usually CR sends such instructions in one
envelope if there are several on hand, but it
does not hold up mailing individual instructions
specifically for that purpose.
FA has drafted a telegram to Tokyo (which
shoula reach FE today) asking for the Ambassa-
dor's comments or suggestions in regard to the
feasibility of extending the present courier
service (Tokyo - Shanghai - Peiping) to Manila,
by one of the following alternative methods:
(1) By using the same personnel as at
present, i.e., Army, Navy and Foreign Service
officers interchangeably; or
(2) by using professional couriers sent
out from the United States.
In as much as the proposed extension of the
present courier service may not be effected for
several weeks, even if travel facilities are
available,

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
Allable, and in view of the confidential
pharacter of such instructions as that under
reference, it would seem that it might be
advieable to send instructions of this character
in the Tokyo pouch, which goes by American
vessel to Shanghai and thence to Tokyo by
courier (if travel facilities are available).
The next Tokyo pouch, closing here Friday,
August 22, is scheduled to leave San Francisco
on the Ss President Monroe on August 29.

4

addressed to Mr. Hornbeck and Mr. Acheson.] At that time
PS/

/SBH

OCT 1 8 1941
keeping this Department informed of action contemplated by
the Standard-Vacuum Company.

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
Today Mr. Walden called from New York to say that
the Shell Company had now cleared its telegram with the
British Embassy and that Mr. Noel Hall had in turn
cleared the sending of the telegram with Mr. Acheson.
Mr. Walden read to me a copy of the proposed Shell tele-
gram which in substance read as follows:
The Shell Company had always considered
the remittance terms of the Japanese Govern-
ment oppressive but it had no alternative if
it wished to make sales at all. However, under
present conditions the Shell Company is not
willing to make further sales until the frozen
balances have been liquidated and provision
made for cash payment. If satisfactory payment
arrangements were agreed to, the Shell Company
thought that it could make shipments of petroleum
products available.
Mr. Walden said that in view of the Shell Company's
aotion, his own company would wish to take a similar
position as a business matter and that since Mr. Acheson
had indicated his approval of the Shell arrangment,
Mr. Walden assumed that there was no objection on the
part of this Government to similar action by the Standard-
Vacuum Company. [Mr. Walden's letter to me of August 21,
>not
which is attached, indicates the total amount of frozen
clian
anter inform
funds which Standard-Vacuum has in Japan. It is my under-
esten induate
totop is ab
standing that this sum is smaller than the amount of the
2,000,00

to

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.99)

past, whereas the Standard-Vacuum balances represents
the full amount of payment for recent shipments only.]
I told Mr. Walden that I had not yet heard from
Mr. Acheson any account of the latter's talk with
Mr. Noel Hafl but that I would get in touch with
Mr. Acheson and call Mr. Walden back.
Subsequently, I was informed by Mr. Acheson that
Mr. Noel Hall had told him of the proposed position of
the Shell Company, that he had understood that this
position represented the positon of the British Govern-
ment which the Shell Company had been told to follow and
that he had not understood that Mr. Noel Hall was sub-
mitting anything for our approval. He said that he had
indicated to Mr. Noel Hall that insistence upon cash
payments for such future deliveries as might be permitted
and liquidation of past balances seemed to be good business
practice. Mr. Acheson did not recall that Mr. Hall had
referred to the last sentence of the proposed Shell message
with reference to possible future aupplies. Mr. Acheson
said that in so far as the Shell message represented the
company n sales policy, he saw no reason for any govern-
mental objection and that similarly there would be no reason
to object to Standard-Vacuum adopting the same kind of policy.
I thereupon

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
I thereupon called Mr. Walden and informed him of
the foregoing. He said that he thought there was no
misunderstanding about the matter, and that both the
Shell's message and a similar message which he proposed
to send to his representatives in Japan related purely
to the companies' sales policies. He went on to say
that the Shell position and the position which his own
company proposed to take meant clearly that as a private
matter they would not sell oil even if permitted to do
so by the Netherlands authorities unless their payment
terms were met. On the other hand, the implication that
they would be willing to continue deliveries if such terms
were met was of course understood to mean sales only of
such products and in such quantities as the Netherlands
authorities might permit.
The next to last message quoted in Mr. Walden's attached
letter of August 21 constitutes his instruction to his
representatives in Yokohama to take the same position as to
payments as the Shell Company.
Mr. Walden referred again to the information which I
had communicated to him as to the Department's position on
August 15 [see my memorandum of conversation with him of
that date] and said that he assumed that we had no objeotion
to his company now applying for licenses to export Diesel oil

!941 SEP 19
My dear Finletter,
You will nedbuhatTtaNSletter I recently wrote to you
about the endeavouraN6f?Eh@RDSanese to borrow peso funds from
the German authorities in Buenos Aires for an equivalent of
7 million yen, in order to make part payment for oxhides bought
in the Argentine.
8
We have now had a most interesting telegram from
6
Santiago of which I am not sending you a copy because it is
badly drafted and couched in unnecessary complicated technical
4
jargon.
Put into plain language the telegram advises us that
4
it was the habit of sellers of mineral products on the entire
West Coast of South America, and prior to the United States
freezing order, to sell against irrevocable U.S. dollar letters
9
of credit before agreeing to put their signature to the indiv-
8
idual contracts of sale for metals. The credits were handled
by the Bank of Chile in Chile, the Banco Italiano in Peru, and
in Bolivia by the Bank of Brazil, operating on behalf of the
Yokohuma Specie Bank.
While the transaction was thus financed in U.S.
dollars, and thst was the currency for cll mineral sales to
Japan, it so happened that instead of payment under the
irrevocable letter of credit being made in dollars, payment
was made in local currency at a guaranteed rate of exchange.
This practice resulted in there being in effect two types of
P
dollar, the U.S. dollar "nd the Japanese dollar, the latter
S
standing at about a 50% discount over the former.
S
Now for the point of this story. The U.S. freezing
B
order has had the curious consequence of creating a ney currency
unit for metal transactions with the Japanese on the fest Coast,
and instead of irrevocable U.S. dollar letters of credYt bing

payment again being made in local currency at a guaranteed

The foregoing is mainly of academic interest but
I pass it on to you because it occurs to me the argument may
one day be advanced that since the U.S. freezing order has gone
into effect it is no longer necessary to try and prevent the
Japanese from securing some of the available minerals on the
West Cost of South America. Of course the fact that our
and your supply requirements of these metals are now so great
will serve as the main rebuttal of such an argument, but it
occurs to me that you might be interested to be able to add
the foregoing as well.
Stopford will let Cochran at the Treasury know
about this also.

JAPANESE NAVAL OIL STORAGE
The following estimates of Japanese Naval Oil Storage
facilities are submitted. The estimates have been prepared
after considerable research and after consulting with the
British Naval Attache in Tokyo and are believed to be reasonably
accurate. For comparison, past estimates are included, the
figures being placed in parenthesis.
It is reliably reported that all naval oil tankage is kept
filled to capacity, and therefore, the Japanese have at the
very minimum the amounts which are indicated:

Total stocks on hand in Japan are variously estimated. A
reliable Dutch source, in close touch with tne Japanese estimates
crude oil in storage to be 6,000,000 tons, but admits that this
may be an under-estimate. The British estimate is 10,786,000
tons as of December 1940. It is probable that the figure 10,000,-
oo0 would be reasonably accurate. Present &nnual consumption,
according to the Dutch is approximately 5,O00,000 tons of orude
oil and 940,000 tons of gasoline.

FAn EASTE AFFAIRS
andd
The honorable Cordell Hull,
1941
Secretary of State,
Washington, D.C.
Octobe
Departmert of State
Dear Sir:
In following local news reports I notice that oil shipments are being made to
Japan by Mexico and South American countries. I further understand that this
action has been carried on without objections from the United States.
In view of our policy of cutting off complete trade relations with Japan,
it seems to me that the above mentioned transactions are unfair to the Amerioan
businessman depending upon Japan for non-combat materials. If allowing this
oil to be shipped to Japan is our way of easing Japanese differences, I feel
that American concerns should also be considered.
There are many items of Japanese manufacture that are not of a military nature.
Due to present relations, these are not being shipped. While I am lo0% American,
a true Demoorat and in full accord with your actions in behalf of our cauntry,
I do feel that the many small business houses that depend upon Japanese goods
should be given every consideration.
In our own oase, we have merchandise ordered late last year that is being held
in Japanese ports. We have cash deposits involved with the balance being covered
by American importers. Restrictions against its delivery is not freczing any
Japanese capital as they have already received their payment. We therefore are
foroed to suffer due to a ruling of our own making.
OCT 1 8 1941
I understand that you are scheduled to meet soon with special representatives
coming to the United States from Jepan via Mexico. Regardless of the outoome
of your meeting with them and the actions that follow, I know that all businessmen
join with me in backing you lo0%. However, I am writing you to express my
personal feelings in hopes that you will give us every consideration.
I realize that these are trying times and that you are doing everything within
your power to close that gap now existing between our two nations. My hope
is that you are successful in your future undertakings.
Harold R Rice

894.24/1623
The Ministry of Economic Warfare informs us that they
received information ohat on Septenver 3, MITSUBISHI informed their
Buenos Aires branch that they had been enquiring in Santiago for l0o0
tons of TARAPACA POTASSIUM CHLORIDE but had learned that the Chilean
Government had informally asked producers to reserve their future
output for South America. MITSUBISHI therefore suggested that their
Buenos Aires office should buy from Chile for shipment to China or
Japan.
Our Ambassador at Santiago has been instructed to take
a suitable opportunity of suggesting to the Chilean Government that
they satisfy themselves that exports to other South American countries
are not for re-exportation.
The Ministry of conomic Warfare ask us to make it clear
ihat the source from which this information is derived, is most
delicate, and should on no account, be compromised. I hope, therefore,
that you will be able to treal this inrormation as most confiaential.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Mitle I am neither seeking nor expecting a personal answer I would
ciate a reply reflecting his thinking relative to two problems which will
hongst those raised in discussion groups I will be leading during the
mmsearanotnes questions, they have been raised many times by
ams and radio but I have never seen the administration's answer.
1. Why do we continue to send war supplies to Japan? We are told that
alortage of steel and gasoline and other war materials are impending. Yet
within a forghtnight these have been loaded a Puget Sound ports. Japan's
Pimite alliance with Germany appears to the best of our kmowledge to be
mding (and insidious). To Japan's attempt to master China has been added
he threat of cambination with the Nazi regime of Europe. We pledge assis-
tanoo to China and then arm the aggressor. I am interested in the reasons,
ibical or expedient, which in the opinion of the Administration justify
apparently contradictory action and philosophy?
2. Why has Germany's attack on Russia changed the truth or error of
Stalin's brand of Communism and the "subservise" activities which have supported
it? Because the Nazi Beast has now chosen to attack the Russian Bear, are we
ssured that if we act to save the Bear the Bear will not"liquidate" us? - etc
NOV 1 8 1941
I am tremendously interested in the answer to thiese. My confession that
can conjure up no legitimate reason - why? - will not, I hope, blind me to
a sympathetic hearing to reasons given. This is one of the few times in my
leadership when I find myself unable to give what appear to me to be
acional answers to both sides of issues about which there remain division of
opinion. I would be reluctant to ask this favor for me alone, but in my
capacity of leader of scores of discussions on current problem I meet these
questfon continuously.
Sincerely yours,
Marvin R. Schafer
College of Puget Sound
Tacoma, Washington.
("Federal Forum" leader for two years)

894.

OCT 3 - 194
to take vis-à-vis Japan. When Mr. Acheson had conoluded,
FILED
Mr. Hornbeok asked Mr. Hall whether he could inform us
what steps the British Government intends to take. Mr. Hal
Jonfidential
replied that his Government expected to denounce certain
commeroial treatiee; that it expected to "tighten controls"

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.98)

asked for details about trade controls and mentioned
certain features of our theory and practice. Mr. Hall
did not elucidate. Mr. Hall stated that the United
Kingdom trade with Japan was no longer important in
volume but that the trade of the Dominions and Colonies
with Japan was important.
On the whole, what Mr. Hall said was inconclusive
and not very comprehensive.
Comment: In the light of this conversation and the
preceding recent conversations which I have had with
Mr. Hall on the subject of coordination of U. S. and
British plans and procedures, I am of the impression that
STRICLCLCCONI
the British Government has rather vague and rather limited
intentions as regards economic pressures against Japan,
and that the British Government's chief interests as re-
gards such conversations is to find out what this Govern-
ment has in contemplation and have itself in position to
offer to this Government suggestions or advice.

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.98)
Mr. J. W. Auchinclbss
-2-
September 19, 1941
The question of Tantalite is also, I think, of interest. I
understand that Tantalite Ore is produced chiefly in Australia, the
Belgian Congo, Uganda and Brazil, and that it has an important use as a
metal and as tantalum carbide which is one of the hard carbides used for
tipping tools. Tantalite is not, I think, one of the commodities which
the United States Government is purchasing in Brazil and wuile Columbite,
an associated metal, is covered by the Brazilian export licencing
regulations, tantalite does. not appear to be so mentioned. We have
received information of exports of tantalite from Brazil to Japan amounting
to 17.2 tons during January - August, 194l. I understand this is quite a
substantial figure from the point of view of inuustrial use. Perhaps,
therefore, you will think that the question of tantalite is also worth
investigation.
Yours sincerely,
R. Reit Adan
R. Reid-Adam.

The following, for your information, is an excerpt from a memo-
randum of a telephone conversation on September 18 between Mr. Fox
of Foreign Funds Control and Mr. Singer of Standard Vacuum:
894.24/1738
"Mr. Singer referred to NY 235383 and NY 237869, cov-
oring $557,000 remittance from Japan and payment of
$105,000 for oil shipment from N.E.I. to Japan. He then
inquired as to the status of these applications. Mr.
Singer was informed that these applications were receiv-
ing the consideration of the Treasury Department and that
he would be informed when a decision had been reached. In
respect to the $557,000 remittance from Japan, Mr. Singer
stated that this remittance had been ordered prior to the
despatoh of their wire, indioating that Standard would con-
sider further shipments only after payment of old indebted-
ness had been made. This is at variance with our previous
understanding whioh was that this remittance followed des-
patoh of Standard's wire by about seven days."
"With respeot to the shipment of oil from the N.E.I.
note

approval of the application covering the financing of the
lie ifthe 4.5.
