SIR:
I have the honor to refer to my telegram of
October 5/7:00 a.m. and subsequent messages relating
to an attempt to ship flasks of mercury on the Japan-
ese vessel AZUliA iARU concealed in iron drums mani-
fested as containing bronze, and in this connection
to report further as follows.
At 1:00 a.m. in the morning of 0ctober 5, 1941
894.24
the undersigned officer made a routine inspection of
the goods which were to be embarked on the AZUMA MARU.
While making this inspection it was notiged that at
this early hour one of the local customs house brokers
was unloading a shipment of iron drums from a freight
car to a barge. Although it was known that these
drums supposedly contained bronze, the fact that it
took four men to lift a drum onto a wheelbarrow plus
the fact that Mr. Llorens (the customshouse broker)
FILED
was personally supervising the unloading seemed strange.
A few minutes later another local customshouse6
19
broker informed me that he had been approached with an
Oonfidential F
offer of 25,000 pesos to ship mercury flasks out in
boxes of resin. The informant stated that the offer
had been made in an unsigned letter and that later a
Japanese from Mexico City named Nagabuchi repeated the
offer over the phone by referring to this letter.
Upon the strength of these additional facts, I,
1942
accompanied by the British Vice Consul (who had received
much the same information), awakened the chief of
customs and informed him that I was certain thet-an
attempt was being made to ship. contraband on the AZUMA
MARU. Mr. Terminel (chief of customs) immediately
stated that he would suspend all exportations until
after

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
after an inspection in the morning. He dressed and
went down personally to give the orders to suspend
all movements, and then cited all the interested
parties for an inspection at 8:00 a.m.
In the morning Mr. Terminel gave orders to open
as many of the boxes and drums as was necessary to
satisfy myself and the British Vice Consul that no
contraband was in the lot. No evidence of illegal
goods was found in either the turpentine or the resin,
but upon the breaking open of the first drum supposed-
1y containing bronze, five flasks of mercury were dis-
covered. While hammering on the second barrel a mer-
cury flask was broken open and the contents spilled.
Immediately, this whole shipment was declared
embargoed, and ordered returned to the customshouse
for opening. To date the task of cutting open the
347 drums has not been completed, and therefore the
total number of mercury flasks contained therein is
not available. However, it seems reasonable to expect
that the majority will contain mercury.
Mr. Antonio Llorens was the local shipper of the
goods, and the bronze was manifested as having been
shipped to Manzanillo from Mexico City by Ramon Obregón
Insurgentes # 430 (148 drums) and Luis Basuto, Palma
#34 (199 drums). However, it is believed that these
last two are fictitious names and that the real person
behind this transaction is Mr. A. Z. Phillips of
Mexico City. The possiblity also arises that Phillips
is working in combination with Nagabuchi, the Japanese
previously mentioned in this despatch. Llorens pro-
fesses complete ignorance of the fact that the ship-
ment contained mercury, and while not many persons are
inclined to believe his story, there seems quite a
possibility that he may be able to escape punishment
this way. Phillips has shipped a great deal of mer-
cury as well as other strategic materials in the past
through this port to Japan and Jianchuko, and has used
Mr. Llorens as his broker in most instances. Phillips
has a bad reputation in this port, and it is understood
that he has also been under suspicion in Mexico City.
Both the customshouse broker named in paragraph
three of this despatch and the local acting manager
of the NYK Line have stated that Phillips was involved
in the transaction.
In view of this discovery it seems probable that
mercury also went out on the Japanese vessel HEIYo
MARU when it sailed from this port on September 18,
1941, inasmuch as 150 drums of bronze were shipped by
A. Z. Phillips consigned to order of shipper at Shanghai.

with copies of newspaper articles relating to the pre-
sent shipment as well as a photograph of the unopened

EECELSIOR
Mexico, D. F.
October 6, 1941.
CONTRABANDO PARA EL EJF DESCUBRESE EN MANZANILLO
Manzanillo, Col., octubre 5. Acaba de ser descubierto
en este puerto un fuerte contrabando de plomo y mercurio
que se trataba de embarcar en el vapor japonés "Azuma Maru".
Agentes aduanales, al servicio de los totalitarios,
trataban de onvertir este puerto en lugar de abastecimientos
para el Eje, a espaldas de las aut oridades y violando los
compromisos internacionales de Mexico.
Los consulados inglés y americano han intervenido
activamente en este asunto, que ha causado gran sensación
en todos los circulos de este puerto.
El Mercurio se Habia Camouflado
En fuentes dignas de todo crédito de esta ciudad, se
nos dieron los siguientes informes sobre el contrabando de
mercurio y plomo a que alude el telegrama de Manzanillo.
En estos momentos se investiga si el caso de contra-
bando a que los referimos formaba parte de un sistema o
programa de contrabando en gran escala y periodico, o si
únicamente se trata de un caso ais lado.
Hace varias semanas que, por decreto presidencial,
consecuencia del pacto comercial firmado entre Mexico y los
Estados Unidos, se suspendieron los envios de mercurio
nacional al Japón, que hasta esa fecha habia sido un
importante consumidor.
Por lo que hace al contrabando descubierto, las
autoridades portuarias de Manzanillo han revelado, segun
nuestros informantes, que el mercurio se encontraba
"camouflado" entre mercancia varia, en frascos con capacidad
para 76 libras cada uno. Hemos logrado saber que algunos
miembros de la embajada del Japon en Washington, se en-
contraban ayer en Manzanillo, esperando el arribo de un
enviado especial de Tokio en Washington, señor Wakasagu,
quien, segun nuestros informantes, trae un mensaje personal
del Principe Konoye, Premier del Japôn, al Presidente
Roosevelt.
El ministro Wakasagu debia llegar a bordo del transporte
japonés Terukawa. A última hora, empero, se ha sabido aqui
que el mencionado transporte atracô en el puerto de Adapulco
ayer, y que el ministro continuó anoche mismo su viaje a esta
capital.

MAS DE MEDIO MILLON VALE EL CONTRABANDO

October 7, 19
MERCURIAL DESCUBIERTO EN MANZANILLO
Las Secretarias de la Economia y Commnicaciones se
Han Puesto de Acuerdo Para Investigar el Caso, y
Ordenaron que se Hicéeron Pesquisas.
De acuerdo con los datos obtenidos por EXCELSIOR anoche,
a un millon quinientos mil pesos asciende el contrabando de
mercurio descubierto el dia de ayer en el puerto de Manzanillo,
y que ya investigan las secretarias de Economia y de Comuni-
caciones y Obras Publicas.
Se intentaba mandar al Japon el cargamento, a bordo del
transperte-ae ese pais, "Azuma Maru"; pero fué descubierto por
las autoridades portuarias, a pesar de que su existencia se
disimulaba en tambores de petrôleo marcados con la leyenda
"objetos de bronce". Aunque en realidad contenian, cada uno,
cinco frascos de mercurio de 75 libras. Nuestros informantes
manifiestan que el contrabando que trataba de fletarse a bordo
del "Azuma Maru", consistia en 300 tambores de petr6leo.
Toma cuerpo—agregase--la hipôtesis de que la partida des-
cubierta e sólo una fracción de las exportaciones ilegales y
en enorme escala, de mercurio mexicano que subrepticiamente
se manda al pais oriental.
El Haiyu Maru Tambien Lleva Azogue
Se agrega que otro barco japónes el "Haiyu Maru", que
zarpó de Manzanillo el pasado 18 de septiembre, 1lemaba a bordo
150 tambores, tamb1én bajo el subterfugio de encerrar "objetos
de bronce". Nuestros informantes, personas allegadas a las
fuentes oficiales que investigan el contrabando, manifiestan
que existen suficientes datos para suponer que el "Haiyu Maru"
lev6 anclas repleto de azogue.
Las dos secretarias de Estado msmatzahm susodichas, han
ordenado, a las autoridades fiscales de Manzanillo, "ahonden
la investigación y procedan a imponer todo el rigor de la ley,
en los casos que asi lo ameritaren".
Se ha Ordenado La Investigacion
Por acuerdo de la Secretaria de Hacienda y Crédito Publicok
la Dirección General de Aduanas hace ya la investigación del
contrabando, que, segun informes publicados en los diarios
de fecha reciente, fué descubierto en el barco "japonés
Azuma Maru".
En vista de tal información la citada dependencia, por la
vía mas rápida, ordenó al visitador especial de aduanas que se
halla en el puerto de Manzanillo, Col., informe detalladamente
sobre cuanto se relacione con ese particular, a fin de que se
pueda establecer, de manera oficial, lo que haya de cierto en
ello.

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
September 22, 1941
Mr. Secretary:
TRADE WITH JAPAN
There are attached two memoranda which survey the
894.24/1750
policies and practices of the British Empire, the
Netherlands East Indies, and the United States regarding
trade with Japan. Except for iron ore, manganese,
petroleum and cotton (exports of which the British and
Dutch are prepared to prohibit if we do so), the three
countries have decided as a matter of policy to cut off
exports to Japan of products useful from a military view-
point.
The survey indicates that, contrary to our assumption,
we have not gone farther in our restrictions on Japan
OCT:20 1941
than the British and the Dutch. Their policy decisions
are in advance of ours in many respects. Our own policy,
owing in part to the dual control which we exercise ever
trade with Japan through export control and financial
control, 1s obscure and not fully developed.
In so far as the actual treatment of trade with
Japan is concerned, as distinguished from the policy de-
cisions, the situation is about the same in all three
countries. Trade is virtually at a standstill.
The

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
The obsourity of our policy and the complexity of
our financial and export controls have given rise in
recent weeks to increasing uneasiness on the part of the
British and Dutch regarding our intentions. They have
expressed to us their desire for a more definitive but
private statement of our policy, including our views on
the products mentioned above, regarding which they are
2
prepared to take action parallel to ours. In this con-
X
nection, there are attached a letter from Mr. Noel Hall
of September 13, a memorandum from the British Embassy
of September 22, a despatch of September 3 from
Minister Biddle, and two memoranda of conversations with
the Minister-Counselor of the Netherlands Legation.
The British and Dutch have told us that they will
go as far as we will, but that they do not want to get
ahead of us. Today, for the first time, and after years
of effort, we have achieved effective joint action against
Japan. Indecision or the evidence of weakness on our
part will endanger this common front.
Recommendatione
There are certain questions now before us involving
parallel action with the British and Dutch which require
immediate and clear action. The recommendations made
below regarding these questions would require no public
announcements.

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
announcements. In no case would they result in any
actual change in our current exports, which are nil due
to a combination of export control, payment difficulties
and suspension of shipping facilities.
Petroleum
At present no petroleum is moving to Japan from any
of the three countries. However, there are applications
pending before both us and the Dutch which require im-
mediate clarification.
(1) Tentative export quotas (of which the British and
Dutch have been advised) have been worked out by the
United States, but exportation is dependent upon the is-
suance of both export licenses and financial licenses.
There are three export licenses outstanding for petroleum
products valued at $178,000, for which no Treasury licenses
have been granted because of the inability of Japan to
arrange satisfactory methods of payment. The Japanese
have recently informed us that they are not prepared to
meet our suggested methods of payment (use of cash held
in this country by Japan or, possibly, use of concealed
Japanese dollar balances held in South America). They
now suggest that payment be made by the shipment to this
country of gold or of American currency which they have
managed to accumulate in the Far East, particularly in
Shanghai. (Payment in gold is ruled out by the policy
decision

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
decision approved by the President not to permit the im-
portation of gold from Japan). We are under no obligation
to the Japanese to consider either method of payment,
since the only methods discussed with them have been those
which they have now rejected.
(2) American oil companies have applied to the Dutch
for export licenses, but the Dutch have held up issuance
of licenses for the reason that licenses for payment have
not been granted by the United States. Should we grant
these licenses for payment, our action would be inter-
preted by the Dutch as an indication that we want to see
the oil exported.
Knowledge by the Dutch of our tentative export quotas
(which they regard as excessive) and the delay in clarify-
ing our position on the question of payment for both
American and Indies oil has caused them to become in-
creasingly uneasy.
It is recommended:
(a) That we inform the Japanese that the methods of
payment proposed by them are not acceptable.
(b) That we inform the British and Dutch in strict
confidence that if they are prepared to act similarly, we
will not permit further exports of petroleum products.
Iron Ore

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
Iron Ore
Malaya has exported iron ore to Japan for many years.
During the last few years the Philippines have also entered
this trade. Malayan exports in the most recent years have
amounted to about 2,000,000 tons; Philippine exports to
about 1,150,000 tons. Japan also imports fairly sub-
stantial quantities of pig iron from India.
The British state that, through prohibition of night
loading and night clearance, they have reduced exports
from Malaya by about forty percent. A quota of 525,000
tons was fixed for exports from the Philippines for the
last seven months of this year, which is equivalent to
an annual level of 800,000 tons or a restriction roughly
comparable to that on Malayan exports. However, because
of heavy exports during the early months of this year,
Philippine exports for 194l as a whole will show little
or no reduction from the high levels of recent years.
The British have now informed us that they are pre-
pared to prohibit the exportation of iron ore from Malaya
if we will prohibit exports from the Philippines.
It is recommended:
(a) That we agree to the British proposal on condition
that exports of pig iron from India be prohibited.
(b) That we tell the High Commissioner to suspend the
issuance of new licenses pending further instructions,
and

=== Page 296 === (confidence 1.00)
-6
and that we review the situation with regard to any out-
standing licenses.
Cotton
Over a period of recent years, the United States and
India have had roughly equal shares in the Japanese cotton
market, the ratio from year to year depending to a large
extent on price factors. During the past year, exports
of American cotton to Japan were extremely small, while
Indian exports continued at the level of the last few
years.
Sinoe the freezing order, no cotton has been exported
from the United States to Japan, although some has con-
tinued to move to occupied China. Exports from India to
Japan have been limited on a monthly basis to the 1940
level in so far as they were covered by confirmed credits
prior to the freezing order. (Exports to occupied China are
also tentatively limited to the 194o level). These credits
are practically exhausted. The British have offered to
reduce exports to Japan to whatever level (in terms of
value) that we reduce.
It is recommended:
(a) That we accept the British offer and inform them
that we are prepared to withhold Treasury licenses for
the exportation of cotton, except for cotton bought
by the Japanese prior to freezing, the release of which
would be dependent upon arrangement being made for the
reciprocal

=== Page 297 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
reciprocal release of American-owmed goods by Japan.
(b) That we approach the British with a view to work-
ing out an arrangement for restricting exports of cotton
to occupied China to reliable British, American and
Chinese mills.
I also recommend that, with reference to each of
these products, we inform the British and Dutch that if
future events should warrant any changes in our policy,
we would discuss the changes with them in advance of
taking action.
In conclusion,.I should like to emphasize again my
feeling that, unless we give the British and Dutch a
clear statement of our policy and take immediate and
decisive action on the questions now before us, the whole
program of concerted action against Japan will be
seriously imperiled and, in my opinion, if it collapses
it can, in all probability, not again be resurrected
within the foreseeable future.
Dean Acheson

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
September 22, 1941
RESTRICTIONS ON JAPANESE TRADE AND PAYMENTS IN
THE BRITISH EMPIRE
All British countries have frozen Japanese assets.
According to the British Embassy, all have agreed in
prinoiple to the policy adopted by the British Government
of restricting exports to Japan to the minimum level neces-
sary to obtain essential imports from Japan.
894.24/1750
As a practical matter trade between the British
Empire and Japan is virtually at a standstill.  Except for
manganese and iron ore from Malaya and cotton from India,
exports to Japan of products valuable from a military view-
point have been completely cut off.
Considerable detailed information is available regard-
ing the measures taken with regard to Japanese trade and
payments by the British Government. It is stated by the
British Embassy that the British Government has recommended
to the Dominions, India, and Burma that they adopt measures
similar to those of the British Government. There is
given, on the following pages, a statement in outline form
of measures taken by the British Government regarding
Japanese trade and payments and, in a parallel column, the

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)

Canada
Blocked Accounts.
Assets of residents of Japan have been frozen.
Accounts of Japanese nationals in Canada (of whom there
are about 20,000, mostly fishermen) have not been frozen,
but consideration is being given to imposing limitations
on withdrawals, partly because of the measures applied
to Canadians in Japan.
Trade in General.
Trade with Japan is at a standstill. The Canadian
Legation states that Canada is cooperating fully with the
UnitedStates and the British Government in restricting
trade with Japan. Restriction of Canadian trade with
Japan has been effected not only by the measures under-
taken by the Canadian Government, but also by the operation
of our own freezing, since Canadian trade with Japan is
conduoted in United States dollars.
Exporte.
Exports had been restricted, prior to freezing, to
products not useful from a military viewpoint and only
one small cargo of wood products and paper ($19,0o0 in
value) has left since freezing.
All exports to Japan are subject to export licenses.
The

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
-8
The only licenses outstanding at the time of the freezing
order, for 7,000 tons of wheat, have been recalled. Li-
denses are being granted only for the personal effects of
persons returning to Japan.
Importe
There have been no imports from Japan since the
freezing order. Lic enses for imports will be granted
only for products needed for military purposes, and it is
believed that the requirements for such products will be
virtually nil.
Trade with Occupied China
A certain amount of miscellaneous cargo is being
permitted to go to Shanghai and some wheat has been ex-
ported to Tientsin. Control is exercised by refusal of
shipment to undesirable consignees, reports being obtained
from the Canadian Trade Commissioner in Shanghai.
No special measures have been taken with regard to
imports from China.
Australia
Trade with Japan is at a standstill. One vessel has
come to Australia since the freezing order. It was per-
mitted to discharge its cargo and take on a cargo of wool
of equal value. Apparently the wool had been purchased
prior to the freezing order, although this is not entirely
clear.
New Zealand

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
New Zealand
The statement received from the New Zealand Govern-
ment by the British indicates that it may not regard any
imports from Japan as essential.
South Africa
The statement received by the British from the Govern-
ment of South Africa implies that no imports are regarded
as essential. The Department has been informed by the
British Embassy that South Africa is withholding licenses
for the export to Japan of fluorspar and "all other im-
portant commodities".
India
Aside from exports of coir and cotton, trade is at
a standstill. Exports of cotton covered by pre-freezing
confirmed credits are being permitted within the limits
of the 1940 level. These credits are now practically
exhausted.
Burma
While no imports from Japan are considered essential,
the Governor has stressed in his reply to the British
Government the need for export outlets.
Eire
All exports go through the United Kingdom and are

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
MB. ACHESON
ADVISER ON POLITICAL RELATIONS
September 20, 1941.
RESTRICTIONS ON JAPANESE TRADE AND PAYMENTS IN THE NETHERLANDS
EAST INDIES.
1. Present factual situation. In fact trade is at a standstila
and all Japanese funds have been frozen. Apparently permission 1s
00
94.24/17
granted for withdrawals for local transactions by Japanese resident
in the Indies.
2. Policy decisions thus far made by the Indies Government.
The Government of the Netherlands Indies has not definitively
59
adopted a detailed policy because we have as yet been unable to in-
form it that we have adopted a definitive policy.* It has assured
us that it desires to adopt a policy no less restrictive than
58
whatever policy we adopt. Like the British Government it has stated
its need for assuring itself of certain types of imports--especially
of cotton textiles which, incidentally, may be obtainable from the
United States. The Indies Government is also desirous of obtain-
ing certain specific lots of goods purchased and already paid for
which the Japanese Government is holding in Japan. It has, in ad-
dition, made specific proposals to us as to what restrictions should
in its opinion be adopted. (See next three paragraphs.)
3. Specific policy proposals made by Indies Government.
ABC
*The Dutch have of course been told what our policy is to date
in theory and in actual practice. As they have, however, had no
P
they have been faced for some weeks past with the issue (whioh we
have not yet had to consider) of whether exports to Japan of import
products shall in fact be permitted. Not wishing to permit such
exports while we are not in fact exporting to Japan they have so far
refused to grant export licenses for products of importance.

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
As early as August 4 the Governor General of the Indies set
forth in some .detail proposals for a restrictive policy.
The Netherlands Legation believes that Mr. Loudon informed the
Under Secretary, the Secretary and Dr. Hornbeck of these
proposals on August 4, August 8 and August 9, respectively,
but the Department has no record thereof. In this memorandum
the general principle is enunciated that "all supplies
enlarging or maintaining Japan's military machine" should
be reduced as far as possible. It is then specifically
stated that such a policy would mean the embargo of all
exports of: bauxite, manganese, palm oil, rubber, tin,
resins, Kapok, nickel ore, salt, rope fiber, scrap iron,
tanning materials and two other less important products
(ilmenite, which is a mineral containing iron and titanium,
and ajarakpits, which are fruit pits of unknown importance).
It is further stated that exports of coffee, lumber, maize,
cassave, cotton (of which the Netherlands East Indies are
negligible producers), sugar and some minor items could be
restricted to normal amounts. On the assumption that it
should be considered undesirable entirely to cut off Japan's
oil supply, it is stated that oil exports could be restricted
to the 1935-1936 level, which in the case of the Indies
it is stated would mean a fifty per cent reduction. Light
crude oil and gasoline of 69 octane content and higher
should

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
should be embargoed and restriotive quotas established for
diesel oil, fuel oil and gasoline. Exports of other non-
military supplies, including copra, could be restricted
to 1935-1936 levels or on some other basis. [Note. The
Legation has informed the Depertment that the memorandum
under reference sets forth the personal views of the Governor
General rather than official proposals of the Indies Govern-
ment, but that in view of the Governor General's position it
may be assumed that the Indies Government is prepared to adopt
the policy described.]
4. Petroleum (further specific proposals). On
September 15 the Legation informally notified the Department
that it believed that the Indies Government 1s prepared to
embargo all exports of petroleum to Japan if the United
States adopts a similar policy. (This had also been intimated
to Mr. Acheson earlier in his discussions with the Legation.)
On September 20 the Legation informed the Department that its
opinion on the matter of a total embargo had been confirmed
by the Indies Government although it had not been instructed.
to make any proposal on this matter to the Department. The
on September 15
Legation further stated/that if exports of oil are not to be
entirely out off the Indies Government is prepared to reduce
1ts quotas below the 1935-1936 level by more than one fourth.
(The Legation had previously notified the Department that
adoption

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
adoption of the 1935-1936 level--which would apparent1y not
result in restriction of United States exports of diesel oil
fuel oil and crude oil--would not be as restriotive of United
States exports of petroleum to Japan as it would be of Indies
exports.)
5. Petroleum (special statement of Indies policy).
The Department has been informed (ina telegram from Mr. Grew,
Tokyo's number 1310 of August 26, 9 p. m.) that on July 28
Mr. Van Hoogstraten of the Netherlands Indies Government
informed the Japanese Consul General at Batavia that the
Indies Government feele that the presence of Japanese troops
in Indochina is a threat to the Indies and that, consequently,
relations between the Indies and Japan have been completely
altered. He stated that thereafter no exports of oil to
Japan will be permitted unless (1) Japan publicly declares
that it has no intention of attacking the Indies and (2)
Japanese troops are withdrawn from southern Indochina. He
said further that if these two conditions are fulfilled, the
Indies Government will still allow only such types of oil
to be exported to Japan as will not increase Japan's offensive
military power.
6. Desire of Indies Government for political assurances.
Estimate of policy Indies Government is prepared to adopt.
Unlike the British Government, the Government of the
Netherlands

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
Netherlands East Indies has indicated that it believes that
discussion of and agreement upon related political issues
should accompany any final decision to adopt a definitive
policy of restriction toward Japan. The Legation has, however,
in recent weeks limited itself primarily to attempting to
ascertain the details of our definitive policy while assuring
us of the desire and intention of the Indies Government to
adopt a closely similar policy. It would appeer that,
regardless of the absence of political commitments, the
Indies are in fact prepared to pattern their policy closely
after whatever policy we adopt and that they believe it wise
to adopt a policy designed severely to restrict all supplies
having military or general industrial value. It is not believed
feasible to obtain more definite indications of Indies polioy
until we are in a position to propose a detailed policy for
adoption by us and by them.

DEPARTMENT OF COMMERCE
HiloptlamassiaRs. Datey 243/73
WASHINGTON
September 24, 1941.
TRADE OF THE UNITED STATES WITH JAPAN, CHINA, HO: KONG AND KWANTUNG,
JUNE 1941 AND SIX MONTHS ENDING JUNE 1941
Prepared in Far Eastern Unit in Cooperation with
Coordination of International Statistics Unit.
(The following statistical compilation with interpretative conment
on United States Trade with Japan, China, Hong Kong and Kwantung is
prepared for the information of Government Officials only and is not
to be disseminated either in whole or in part to outside interests)
JAPAN
Exports from the United States to Japan continued in a downward trend in
June with shipments valued at only $5,687,000 against $6,623,000 in May and
$15,095,000 in June 1940. Exports were confined almost exclusively to raw
cotton, valued at $1,580,c00, and petroleum products amounting to $3,469,000.
Machine tools, iron and steel products, and other metals essential in the
United States defense progran, and formerly shipped in large quantities to
Japan, were completely erased from the export list.
In the first six months of this year, our exports to Japan amounted to
only $53,032,000, or nearly 55 percent under the January-June 1940 total of
$106,965,000. Raw cotton, valued at $25,478,000 in the 1940 half year period,
dropped to a value of $4,762,000, this year. Iron and steel semimanufactures
totaled only $165,000 in the first six months of this year, against $15,174,000
in the corresponding period of l940, while metal-working nachinery was valued a
$837,000 against $15,488,000 in the first half of 1940. Sizeable recessions
occurred also in exports of refined copper, ferro-alloys, automotive products
and ammonium sulphate. The only notable increase in the half year trade was
a $6,000,000 gain in exports of petroluem products. Although exports of
25946

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
of aviation gasoline and crude petroleum deolined, shipments of other gasoline
and lubricating oil were substantially higher than in the first half of l940.
In contrast to the decline in export trade, imports into the United
States from Japan moved forward during June to a value of $13,000,000 from
$10,869,000 in May, and were considerably higher than the June 1940 total of
$8,972,O00. a lerge part of the June increase was accounted for by speculative
buying of raw silk, importers laying: in stocks in view of disturbed American-
Japanese trade relations. Silk imports were valued at $9,226,000 against
$7,879,000 in May and $5,273,000 in June 1940.
General imports from Japan for the first half of 1940 were still some-
what below the January-June 1940 figure, amounting to $63,894,000 and
$66,542,000 repsectively. Imports for consumption, however, totaled
$65,648,000 compared with $66,348,000 in the 1940 period. Raw silk imports
were slightly higher at $43,186,000 against $41, 294,000 in the first half of
last year, and accounted for 65 percent of the import trade. Small increases
were registered also in purchases of bleached cotton cloth, mink furs, tea
and refined camphor. On the other hand, inports of crabmeat and tuna fish
declined to comparatively small amounts.
The upward tendency in imports coupled with the sharp recession in
exports, resulted in an import excess of $10,862,000 in our trade with Japan
in the first half of this year. This compares with an export excess of
$40,423,000 in the trade during the corresponding period of 1940.
CHINA
Ever since March of this year, exports from the United States to China
have each month exceeded exports to Japan, normally much the larger market.
June shipments to China totaled, however, but $6,055,000, compared with
$12,122,000 in May, the decline being the result largely of depressed
25946

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.97)
8
一3-
conditions in the Shanghai market and to United States export control measures.
Notwithstanding this severe decrease, June exports to China exceeded exports
to Japan by $368,000.
Exports from the United States to China for the first half of the year,
valued at $47,641,000, about equalled the value of the trade in the corre-
sponding period of 1940, and were only $5,391,000 under the half-year value of
exports to Japan. This is in sharp contrast to the first six months of 1940,
when our exports to Japan exceeded United States shipments to China to the
extent of nearly $60,000,000.
United States Exports to China by Designated areas
Beginning with January 194l, the Coordination of International Statistics
Unit of the Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce has compiled statistics of
United States exports to China by designated areas; namely, "Free" China,
Shanghai, and "Occupied" China. Sumnary tables of the trade for the first
11-14
half of 1941. will be found on pages 12 15 of this report, following the table
of principal exports to all China. Conparable figures of the trade by
designated areas for 1940 are not available.
These tables indicate that 53.3 percent of the June (1941) exports to
China from the United States went to "Free" China, while Shanghai accounted for
37.6 percent. The respective ratios for the first half year were 48.3 percent
and 41.8 percent, with Japanese-controlled areas accounting for only 9.3
percent of the total exports.
Consumers' goods, notably tobacco, wheat flour, paper, raw cotton and
petroleum products, bulk large in the trade with Shanghai, while exports to
"Free" China consist primarily of aircraft and parts, motor trucks and busses,
non-ferrous metals, petroleum products, explosives and ammunition.
25946

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Imports from China
Difficulty in getting goods out of China in view of the Japanese blockade
was in part responsible for the decline in June imports into the United States
from China to a value of $7,078,000 from $8,618,000 in May. Tung oil, tin,
tungsten, antimony and raw silk were among the principal commodities in the
trade registering recessions.
General imports from China in the first half of this year, however,
valued at $43,693,000, were only slightly under the January-June 1940 total of
$43,822,000. Bristles, raw silk and tungsten showed increases, but sizeable
declines occurred in imports of tung oil and tin.
HONG KONG AND KWANTUNG
There was considerable improvement in United States exports to Hong Kong
in the first half of this year when the total reached a value of $14,900,000,
compared with $9,049,000 in the corresponding period of 1940. Machinery and
vehicles, chemicals, and miscellaneous items accounted for most of the gain.
Trade with Kwantung continued depressed as a result of the restrictions
imposed in the Manohurian area. Expcrts to Kwantung in the first six months
of this year totaled $1,873,000 against $4,394,000 in the same period of 1940,
while imports were valued at $1,100,000 and $692,000, respectively. The
increase in imports resulted from larger purchases of Manchurian magnesite and
perilla oil.
Far Eastern Unit,
VCHungerford; mk
Septomber 24, 1941.

Memorandum of Conyersan
ands East Indies Policy in Regard to Restriet
on Trade with Japan
Baron van Boetzelaer,
Minister Counseloror
the Netherlands
Alger Hiss
Assistant Secretaryq
Division of
of State
COPIES TO:
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
1753
SEP 19 1941
FE
OCT 2 0 1941
Department of St
MR. ACHESON
Baron van Boetzelaer came in to see me again at his
request. He said that he was uhder considerable:pressure
both from the Netherlands Government in London, and from
894,
the Netherlands Indies Government to obtain more definite
Confidentiar
information as to the policy of the United States with
respect to restrictions on trade with Japan. He said
that his Government felt that it must make some definite
decision soon, that it had for some weeks past anticipated
ALT
receiving almost momentarily a final statement of our
policy, particularly with respect to exports of petroleumo
161
products, and that his Government was especially anxious
that they not take any action which they might later find
was

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
was more liberal than the policy which the United States
would ultimately adopt. He said that, of course, his
Government also would not wish to adopt more restrictive
measures than those of the United States, but that the
concern thus far
expressed had been lest the Neth-
erlands adopt more liberal policies because of inadequate
information as to the policies of the United States. Such
a development, he said, his Government would consider most
unfortunate.
I told Baron van Boetzelaer that, as I had said to
him on other occasions recently, I was in no position to
give him any more detailed information than Mr. Acheson
or I had previously given to him, but that I would again
inform Mr. Acheson of the importance whioh the Netherlands
Government ascribed to an early clarification of this
matter.
Baron van Boetzelaer then said that he had the
preceding evening gone over the Legation's files again,
and he had found that the Department had already been
given a rather clear indication of the Indies policy in a
number of respects and that he thought that by referring
back to these previous statements he would be able to
clear up a number of the inquiries which Mr. Acheson and I
had

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.98)
the dates specifed a atanytine
by the nottikass minesty on the date specfed but no mens
mad made of him as to Netherlands policy. He than pro-
ceeded to read from a memorandum of August 4 whioh had
been received by the Legation from the Governor-General
of the East Indies. Baron van Boetzelaer said that this
memorandum had been shown to Mr. Welles by the Netherlands
Minister on August 4, had been disoussed with Dr. Hornbeok
on August 8
and with the Secretary on August 9. Baron van Boetzelaer
was not sure whether a copy of the memorandum had actually
been left with any of these officers. He then read
portions of the memorandum which he said were still
relevant to the questions under consideration, and per-
mitted me to make rather full notes. In substance, those
portions of the memorandum which he read were as follows:
(1) Japanese funds in the Indies are to be con-
centrated in a single account and are to be frozen
primarily as a means of protecting the Netherlands'
interests in purchases which had already been made in
Japan and already paid for, but not yet delivered. These
funds will be released for use by Japan in paying for oil
and other Indies products, so far as the Indies Government
may grant permission for the exportation of suoh products,
in proportion to releases by Japan of the Netherland
purohases referred to above.
(2) In

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.99)
(2) In.view of the danger to the Netherland Indies
resalting from the occupation of Indo-China, all supplies
enlarging or maintaining Japan's military machine should
be reduced as far as possible. Such a policy would mean
cutting off all exports of bauxite, manganese, palm oil,
tin, rubber, resins, Kapok, tanning materials, nickel ore,
ilmenite, salt, djarakpits (a fruit pit), rope fiber, and
scrap iron. Normal exports could be licensed of coffee,
lumber, maize, cassave, cotton, sugar, and some minor items.
not
If it should/be considered desirable to cut off entirely
Japan's oil supply, exports of oil could be restrioted to
the 1935 - 1936 level which would result 1n a reduction by
fifty per cent of Indies exports of oil. Light crude oil
and gasoline of more than sixty-nine ootane content should
be excluded altogether. In subdividing any allotted quota
of petroleum products, diesel oil, fuel oil, and gasoline
should also be restricted. Exports of other non-military
goods, including copra, could also be restrioted to
1935 - l936 levels or could be otherwise restrioted.
(3) Prior agreement between the United States, the
British Empire, and the Netherlands Indies is required,
especially as to the possibility that war may result from
such measures. A weak blockade, not backed by the certainty
of concerted further action is dangerous and ineffective.
Such

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
Such a policy would lend fatal support to the impression
not only of the Japanese, but also of other Asiatio
people, (at this point Baron van Boetzelaer said that the
Governor-General especially had the Chinese in mind)that
the United States, the British Empire, and the Netherlands
East Indies do not dare to face the extreme consequenoes
the
of their policy, and as a result instead of stopping/Jap-
anese march to the south, such a policy would enoourage
new aggressions and would possibly weaken Chinese resist-
ance. The deterring effect of the blockade (Baron van
Boetzelaer here observed that the term "blockade" was
inacourately used and that the Governor-General meant the
English equivalent of "embargo") would be entirely lost
and Japanese propaganda would be made much easier. Today
this propaganda is that the interests of Asiatic peoples
are sacrificed to those of the United States, and this
propaganda makes efforts to loosen the tie between the
three empires by pretending that Japanese action aims only
at England and the United States. A merely gradually
tightening blockage (embargo) would mean that Japan gets an
ever stronger foothold in Indo-China whioh makes it
inoreasingly diffioult for her to retrace her steps. A
conflict would become more and more unavoidable. Military
elements in Japan would construe this as a justification
of

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.97)
of their poliey, and on the other hand the possible
resistance of more prudent elements in Japan will become
accordingly weaker. A gradually tightening blockade
(embargo) requires a clear agreement as to the military
consequences which may occur at a moment which is more
unfavorable for the Allies than the present, assuming that
the whole political situation does not change completely
for the better. When a common blockade (embargo) 1s made,
it is necessary to communicate to Japan the conditions
for ceasing the action taken (1. e., the conditions upon
whioh the embargoes will be lightened or removed).
I thanked Baron van Boetzelaer for this additional
information and said that I thought Mr. Acheson would
find it helpful if he had not already received similar
information from Mr. Welles, Dr. Hornbeck, or the Secretary.
I said that I, myself, had not seen a copy of the memo-
randum from which he had read nor such a detailed
exposition of the proposed policy of the Indies.

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
policy which Baron van Boetzelaer had read to me in the
course of his call on September 18. He said that as the
Minister is still out of town he has been unable to ascertain
whether or not a copy of the memorandum was actually left
with any of the officials of the Department with whom the
Minister seid he had discussed the memorandum. In answer to
my request for a further description of the status of the
memorandum, Baron van Boetzelaer eaid that it represented
the personal views of the Governor General which views the
Governor General offered for consideration in discussions
designed to work out policies of the Indies Government and of
the Government of the United States along similar lines.
The memorandum, Baron van Boetzelaer said, wasnot an official
proposal but in view of the Governor General's position it
could be regarded as representing views which the Indies
Government was prepared to express officially if the case
arose therefor. Baron van Boetzelaer went on to say that
what has transpired since August 4, the date of the
memorandum, and information which he had received as to the
attitude of the Indies Government makes him feel sure that
there has been no disposition on the part of the Indies
Government to take any different attitude than that expressed
in the memorandum.
Baron van Boetzelaer

Baron van Boetzelaer subsequently called me again on

Mr. Welles in particular had commented that the memorandum
was most interesting. In discussing the memorandum with the
three officials mentioned above the Minister had pointed out
that the memorandum did not represent official views but
merely the personal feeling of the Governor General. The
Minister had at the same time pointed out that in view of
the Governor General's position his personal feelings were
of course of importance. Baron van Boetzelaer then reaffirmed
his own description to me of the status of the views expressed
in the memorandum as they are outlined in the preceding
paragraph.

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)
Assistant Socretary
PARLIAMENTARY QESTION
of State
OCT 2 - 1941
Anglo-Japanese Trade
MR. ACHESON
30. Lieut.-Colonel Sir Thomas Moore asked the Minister of Economic
Warfare whether, in view of public anxiety, he can make any statement.
on the trade-barter agreement with Janan: and why it is regarded by
His Majesty's Government as necessary?
Mr. Foot: No general barter arrangement, nor anything in-
consistent with the strict enforcement of the freezing Order, has ever
been contemplated. The use of frozen funds for new exports to Japan
is not permitted. If it should be found desirable exceptionally to
license any particular imports from Japan, it would be a matter for
consideration what exports should be allowed against such imports and
how payments should be effected. It is probable that this would best
be done by setting up a separate clearing account, if and when the
need for it should arise. At present, however, no such imports have
been licensed and consequently no question of payments arises.
Sir T. Moore: As Japan is a partner in the Axis, would not
such a proposal be almost equivalent to trading with the enemy? Would
it not be unwise for the Government to be a party in any way to a
transaction with Japan?
Mr. Foot: I think it is clear from the terms of my answer
that no trade is taking place between the two countries at all.
Mr. Noel-Baker: Does that also apply to the Colonial Empire?
Mr. Foot: This question refers only to the United Kingdom.
In these matters the Colonial Empire generally follows suit. Perhaps
my hon. Friend would put a separate Question down.
10th September, 1941.

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.99)
PARLIAMENTARY QUESTION
Assistent Secrotary
of State
OCT 2 - 1941
JAPAN
MR. ACHESON
33. Mr. A. Edwards asked the President of the Board
of Trade the Government's policy with regard to trading
with Japan; and to what extent in the last three months
shipments of materials which can be used for munitions
of war have been made to Japan?
Sir A. Duncan: All Japanese assets in the United
Kingdom were frozen by an Order dated 26th July, and
export of all goods from the United Kingdom to the Japanese
Empire was made subject to licence from 15th August. No
licences have been granted for exports to Japan since the
date of the Freezing Order, and I see no prospect that any
such licences will be granted in the near future. No
materials which, so far as is known, can be used as
munitions of war have been exported from the United Kingdom
to Japan during the last three months.
9th September, 1941.

MR. BERLE
My dear Mr. Berle:
It is confidentially reported that the China Import and Ex-
port Lumber Company and the firm of Jandine and Madison, both of
which are British firms, have been supplying the Japanese Army in
China. In order to get the Japanese Army business, the China Im-
port and Export Lumber Company reputedly must give up about 30%
commission to the Japanese Army men in China. It is alleged that
Mitsubishi and Company, Limited, Mitsui and Company, Limited, and
other Japanese firms have underbid the above mentioned British
firms, but have been unable to secure the business with the Japanese
Army in China.
The China Import and Export Lumber Company is understood to be
owned by Sir Sasoon, a British Jew, who is believed to be residing
in Shanghai, China. The Agent for this company in the Uni ted States
is believed to be Dant and Company, Portland, Oregon. The firm of
Jardine and Madison is said to be represented by one McMillan in
Wancouver, British Colum

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.99)
October 16, 1941
MEMORANDUM
Reports have been received from an outside confidential source
indicating that a Mitsubishi engineer, when consulted as to the size of
valves, et cetera, in connection with some tanks, early in 1940, stated
the finished products were being sent to Tsingtao. This individual also
asserted that his concern had constructed numerous such tanks at Tsingtao
and, further, many unassembled tanks had been purchased in the United States.
This report also indicated that Jyodo Kigyo, as well as Mitsubishi, had
FW 894.24/1764
constructed tank storage at Tsingtao.
Another source of information advised that he had no definite
knowledge of storage facilities at Tsingtao, but stated the Japanese have
built an air field about twelve to fifteen miles northeast of that city, at
which point they have cut into the sides of the hills, either for aircraft
or oil storage.
The following statistics on the oil situation in Japan during the
past few years are reported to be accurate and will form a basis for an under-
standing of the entire situation as regards production, consumption, and in
estimating present reserve stocks.

According to this Bureau's source, the problem of the fuel oil reserve
in Japan was discussed with the British and United States Naval Attaches late in
1939. At that time there was an estimated fuel oil reserve equal to about two
and one-half years' peace time consumption. Presently these reserves are be-
lieved to be enough for about two years' peace time supply. The efficiency in
refineries is believed to be lower than in l939 due to corrosion of steel and
the difficulties of replacing parts. Labor efficiency is also believed to be
lower.
The following details of the aluminum producing firm of Nichito
Kogaku Kogyo K.K. are being set forth as a matter of possible interest:
Factory in Hachinoke City, Aomori Prefecture.
Capital Yen 20,000,000 with Yen 12,000,000 paid up.
Principal Shareholder - Sai Nihon Sato K.K., one of the
Mitsubishi group, holding 135,000 of the 400,000 shares
issued.
Aluminum Output - 1940 - 1,200 Kilo Tons
1941 projected 3,000 Ki1o Tons.
New Material imported from the Netherlands East Indies.
This factory also has a rated capacity for Ammonium
Sulphate of 36,000 Kilo Tons annually.
A report has also been received indicating that Ebihara Jidosha
Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha, which formerly assembled small motorcycles and
lauries, was converted about a year and ahalf ago to war production.
-12-

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.98)
This firm is now engaged in producing naval aero-bomb casings, armored motor-
oyoles, trucks and other armed vehicles. The factory is located at No. 902-
910, Haneda Moto-cho, Kamata-Ku, Tokyo, and employs a maximum of two hundred
In this connection, it has been learned that the Hakodate Dockyard
Company, in April, 1941, increased its capitalization from about Yen 7,000,000
to Yen 25,000,000 for the purpose of constructing a dry dock for 10,000 ton
ships, as weli as for the improvement of and addition to their equipment.
Since the ban on imports of asphalt to Japan more than a year ago,
the use of this product by civil manufacturing plants was denied as from
March, 194l. Considerable supplies of asphalt from imported crude oil are
all being stored for military use. The Japanese Manufacturers of Asphalt
Roofing Association appealed for aid to the Ministry of Commerce, but were
told by the military members of the appeal committee of the Ministry that the
Japanese Army has a more important use for asphalt than the manufacture of
asphalt roofings. It is reported that this important use is believed to be
in connection with the air fields on the Manchukuo and Siberian border and
in the Peiping, North China, area.
Because of their inability to secure electric motors, manufacturers
of air cooling equipment have been unable to produce any new units. The most
important of these manufacturers is the Tokyo Carrier Company of Tokyo, the
sole remaining stocks of which were taken over by the Japanese Navy for use
on hospital ship operating rooms.
Considerable dislocation of cargo handling between Japan and China
is caused by priority being demanded for army shipments. In many reported
cases, ships are fully loaded and ready to sail when the army insists on the
unloading of their cargo in order to make room for army supplies
-13-

Dupartment of State
The legal action which the Japan Gasoline Company is
bringing against the Universal Oil Products Company in-
volves two contracts, the substance of which is summarized
below.
Under one contract dated July 19, l928, the Universal
Oil Products Company agreed to make available to the Japan
Gasoline Company without limitation of time oertain teohni-
cal information and patent rights for the processing or
refining of petroleum within the Empire of Japan for a
consideration of one million dollars. This contract evi-
dently related to processes for the production of motor
PS/MCS
gasoline. Under this contract the Universal Oil Products
Company during 1939 and January 1940 carried on negotia-
tions with Mitsui and Company through the Japan Gasoline
Company to submit drawings and plans for the installation.
C
gasoline.
The

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
The delivery of this information apparently is not
directly subject to the Department's policy which relates
to the delivery of plans, plants, manufacturing rights, or
technical information required for the production of high-
quality aviation gasoline. From the Department's earlier
correspondence with Mr. Halle, it appeared that the Universal
Oil Products Company planned to go ahead with the sale of
the plans for these cracking plants. Mr. Halle had been
informed that it was up to each individual company to
decide whether a particular transaction was or was not sub-
ject to the policy in question. It later developed that
Mr. Halle decided to call off the negotiations for the sale
of this information. He said that he reached this decision
after conversations with officers of the Department who,
according to Mr. Halle, did not definitely state that this
particular transaction was contrary to the Government's
polioy, yet gave the impression that the Department cid
not look with favor upon its consummation. It may be
noted at this point that Mr. Halle has consistently urged
a broadening of the policy to include motor gasoline which
he states is readily stepped up to aircraft grade.
The other contract is one involving the furnishing
of rights, plans, and information for the construction
of an imo-octane plant. The delivery of such information
1s

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
is definitely within the scope of the policy. This con-
tract, dated August 19, 1938, was a result of a dispute
over the inclusion of certain information under the l928
contract, and provided for an additional million dollars
to be paid to the Universal Oil Products Company. Seven
hundred thousand dollars have been paid under this contract,
the remaining $300,000 to be paid upon the delivery of cer-
tain plans and the completion and satisfactory operation
of the plant. The details of both contracts are set forth
in the bill of complaint which is enclosed with Mr. Halle's
letter of June 18.
Under this latter contract, an iso-octane plant has
been partly construoted in Japan, but it cannot be com-
pleted until the Universal Oil Products Company releases
further information. Universal has withheld this informa-
tion in cooperation with the Government's policy. This
partioular contract has been the subject of considerable
discussion with members of the Japanese Embassy. It was
referred to in the Embassy's note of January 6, 1940, and
in conversations with Mr. Morishima, Counselor of the
Embassy, on January 30 and March 5.
y
Harold W. Moseley

dated October 9, 1941 concerning an attempt to ship

anese vessel AZUMA MARU, and in this connection to
report further as follows:
On October 7, 1941 this office was informed by
the Chief of the local freight office that railway
box car number NM 61044 had arrived at Manzanillo
consigned to Sr. Antonio Llorens, manifested as con-
taining 61 drums of bronze. The administrador of the
customshouse was immediately informed, and he promised
to take action in the matter. A few days later the
customshouse advised that there was no car  that
number here.
A check was made with the information on hand
at this office, and it was found that thel eustomsheus
had made an error in transcribing the number of the
car. The correction was made and anotherfnvesig
tion ordered.
RECORD
.On October 13, the customshouse chief informed
this office that the car had been ordered reshipped
to Tampico, and had left Manzanillo October 9 con
signed to Morales, Fernandez y Cia., at Tampico. 
This Vice Consulate immediately telegraphed this
information to the Embassy in Mexico City as wellas
to the Consulate in Tampico, and local Mexican auho-
rities advised their various superior offices.
From press notices received here, it appears
that

being sent to South America to outbid the United

in Brazil in August two Japanese, Hiroshi Yoda and

quartz crystal into little images for the purpose

the shipment of this commodity to areas other than
the United States. The Embassy has reported this
case to the Bank of Brazil, with the request that
exportation be prohibited.

No. 3333
SUBJECT: SHIPMENTS OF MERCURY FROM SPAIN
TO JAPAN VIA ARGENTINA
WS
Strictly Confidential
894.24/1774
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Divicion of
NOV 17 1941
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
V11941
DIVISION OF EXPORTS
The Honorable
AND DEFENSE AID
Department of State
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
With reference to the Embassy's despatch No. 3213
of October 3 last, regarding shipments of mercury from
PT ED
Spain to Japan via Argentina and the arrangements made
for their payment in this city, I have the honor to re-
1941
port that the British Embassy has received a telegram
from the Ministry of Economic Warfare in reply to an
inquiry as to why, in view of our.own Government's efforts
a
/ Carbon copies
doetM.R.m.

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
to curtail the supplying of Japan with Mexican mercury,
the British authorities had issued certificates permitting
this Spanish mercury to be shipped to Japan. A paraphrase
of the pertinent part of the message, which is dated
October 15, is given below:
"The belligerent rights of our Govern-
ment do not, of course, permit us to stop
shipments to Japan from neutral countries in
the absence of evidence that the goods are to
go to the enemy or are to benefit him. We are,
however, fully aware of the importance of ob-
structing to the maximum the shipment to Japan
of merchandise of a vital character from the
Iberian Peninsula. Instructions were sent last
September 3 to the British Ambassadors to Spain
and Portugal to the effect that future applica-
tions for certificates covering articles con-
signed to Japan, whether directly or indirectly,
must be referred to the consideration of the
Ministry of Economic Warfare. It is the inten-
tion of the Ministry to hinder to the very utmost
shipments of this nature by means of administra-
tive delays. Owing, however, to both the afore-
mentioned legal aspect and the relations of the
British Government with Spain, such delays cannot
be prolonged for indefinite periods. The mercury
shipments by the Mar Cantabrico and the Aldecoa
were made prior to the adoption of these measures,
but a quantity of engines by the Mar Caribe are
understood to have been detained under the new
measure at Cadiz."
The British Ambassador has informed this Embassy
that his Government has every wish to cooperate with the
United States Government to the greatest extent possible
in efforts to prevent the shipment of mercury to Japan
and that his Government would welcome any proposals that
our Government would be dispomed to make in regard to
shipments of the article from Spain to Japan.
Subsequent to the sending of the Embassy's despatch
no. 3213 of October 13, it was learned that the 5,000
flasks of mercury which were shipped to Japan from here

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
on August 28 had arrived from Spain on the Aldecoa (2,000
flasks) and the Mar Cantabrico (3,Ooo flasks), and not on
the Mar Caribe, as was reported in the despatch aforemen-
tioned. It is understood that 5,O00 flasks, which ap-
parently arrived on the Mar Caribe, are still here await-
ing shipment.
Respectfully yours,
Copies to the Embassies
at Tokyo and Madrid.
File No. 863.7

SUBJECT:
Exports of iron ore from the Phili
Japan
PARTICIPANTS:

Mr. J. J. Reinstein, A-A

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

ere
1-1408
Mr. Dent came to see me today at the request of
Mr. Hall. He said that the information which had been
given to Mr. Hall by Mr. Acheson on September 27 had been
894.4
transmitted to London and that a telegram had been re-
ceived by the Embassy stating that the status of our
exports of iron ore to Japan was not entirely clear to
the British Government. He said that the British have
been permitting about 20,oo0 tons of ore a month to go
from Malaya to Japan and that they were not certain whether
they should now stop these statements or not. He asked
me if I could clarify the situation for him. I told him
FILEDPS/SMS
that we had received some information from the Philippines
regarding

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
garding the status of the quota and that I would check
with Mr. Acheson and get in touch with him.
After discussing the matter with Mr. Acheson, I
tolephoned Mr. Dent and gave him the following information.
The quota for exports of iron ore from the Philippines
to Japan has not been exhausted. Export licenses covering
about 160,000 tons of ore issued against the balance of
the quota are still outstanding. There is furthermore,
room within the quota for exports in addition to the ore
covered by the outstanding licenses. However, no ore has
actually left the Philippines since August 7 in view of
the failure of the Japanese boats to call at the islands.
I told him that with respect to the part of the quota for
which no licenses had as yet been granted, it was the plan
to have any new applications for licenses referred to
Washington where they would be held. The situation with
regard to the outstanding licenses is being looked into
and we were trying to find some way of dealing with this
aspect of the problem. Mr. Dent said that it appeared in
view of what I had said, that further exports were unlikely.
In reply, I repeated my statement that as to the outstanding
licenses we are looking into the situation to see what can
be done and that as a practical matter no ore 1s actually
J1R
moving because of the shipping situation.
J. J. Reinstein
A-A:JJR:MFA

1782
Baron van Boetzelaer called me on the telephone
and asked me if I had seen an Associated Press story
appearing on page seven of today's Washington Post
under the headline "3 Nations Act to Stop All Oil
to Japan". I replied that I had heard of some such
story but had not yet seen it personally. He then
read to me the first santence of the article which reads
as follows:
"The United States, Great Britain and the
refugee government of the Netherlands, it was
learned authoritatively yesterday, have agreed
to stop all oil shipments to Japan."
00
FILHD
Baron van Boetzela

=== Page 431 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Baron van Boetzelaer said that the Legation
has received a cable from the Netherlands East
Indies to the effect that a similar Reuters dispatch,
which he assumed came from London, has been received
in the Indies but has not been published. The Indies
Government had asked the Legation whether there were
any developments with which the Indies Government was
not familiar. Baron van Boetzelaer said that he would
have to make some reply to this cable today.
I told him that I personally knew of no more
basis for this story than Baron van Boetzelaer himself
does. I said that I would consult my superiors in the
Department and that unless he heard further from me by
the close of business today, he could assume that the
Department has no further information on this matter
than I have.

October 10, 1941
MR. ACHESON
MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION
Mr. Nisiyama called this morning at Foreign Funds
Control, at his request, and was received by Messrs. Pehle
and Fox of Foreign Funds Control, and the undersigned.
Mr. Nisiyama referred to his conversation with Mr.
Fox of October 1, at which time Mr. Nisiyama inquired as to
the result of the Treasury Department's consideration of
the proposal of the Japanese to bring to the United States
gold or currency in payment for pending oil shipments. At
that time Mr. Fox replied that the proposal was still under
894.24/1783
consideration, pending the discussion of the problem before
the Interdepartmental Policy Committee of Foreign Funds Con-.
trol.
Mr. Nisiyama then stated that having had no word from
the Treasury about the previous proposal, it had been decided
by the Japanese Government to use South American bank balan-
894.2
ces in payment for the oil. He presented to Mr. Pehle a memo-
randum, a copy of which is attached, which states that the
position with respect to Japanese dollars in South America
had eased somewhat during recent weeks and that, accordingly,
840.51
arrangements had been made with the Banco de Brasil to trans-
fer certain funds to the Chase National Bank in payment for
the oil.
Mr. Pehle referred to the statement in the memorandum
about the easing of the Japanese dollar position in South
America and asked how this had been brought about. Mr. Nisi-
yama said there were two Japanese vessels with shipments from
Japan en route to the West Coast of South America. In answer
to Mr. Pehle's question as to whether these boats were carry-
43
ing gold, Mr. Nisiyama replied in the negative and stated that
there were only exports from Japan of merchandise on these
boats.
Mr. Pehle then recalled to Mr. Nisiyama that the ques-
OCT 1 8 1941
tion of the use of South American bank balances had first been
raised about six weeks ago and that while the Treasury was
FILHD
considering this problem, Mr. Nisiyama had stated that South
American balances were not available in payment for oil from
this

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
this country. Accordingly, Mr. Pehle stated that the con-
sideration of the use of South American balances had been
dropped, but that such consideration would now be reopened.
*********
本**本***********
Mr. Pehle then stated to Mr. Nisiyama that this Gov-
ernment was somewhat concerned with the situation of the
New York agency of the Yokohama Specie Bank. He said that,
as Mr. Nisiyama knew, this bank had effected substantial
cash withdrawals immediately prior to the freezing order
and also had issued instructions to their correspondents in
South America not to remit proceeds of collections of exports
to South America to the New York bank, as had been the usual
procedure. Mr. Nisiyama stated that he was familiar with
these matters. Mr. Pehle said that these factors, coupled
with the cessation of Japanese exports to this country, had
reduced the availability of dollar assets of the Yokohama
Specie Bank in New York and that the situation was aggravated
by the fact that this bank is the principal channel of re-
mittances from Japan to this country for such purposes as pay-
ment of American creditors for debts incurred prior to the
freezing, service on the Japanese Governmental debt, expenses
of Japanese diplomatic and consular officers and of Japanese
newspaper men, eto. He said that Mr. White, Superintendent
of Banks of the State of New York, had brought to the attention
of this Government the conditions of the Yokohama Specie Bank
in New York and that Mr. White was concerned that withdrawals
from the bank should proceed in a proper manner. Mr. Pehle then
stated that he understood that the officials of the Yokohama
Specie Bank had, within the last few days, taken the position
that the bank would not effect remittances to American mission-
aries in Japan unless free dollar accounts were set up to
the credit of the bank in this country. Mr. Pehle said that in
view of the obvious need of the Yokohama Specie Bank for dollar
assets, the Treasury Department had been surprised to hear that
the bank was insisting on the receipt of free dollars for this
purpose

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
purpose and suggested that Mr. Nisiyama might wish to dis-
cuss this matter with the officials of the Yokohama Specie
Bank. Mr. Nisiyama stated that he was familiar with the
position of the bank but that it was not so very desperate;
he said that the principal liability of the bank vas one of
approximately $6,000,000 incurred in connection with the sale
by American interests of a gold mine in Japan, which liability
matured in instalments over a period of the next two years; in
addition, Mr. Nisiyama said that the bank had available 15 to
17 million dollars of import bills covering silk  warehouses
which bills are being liquidated in good order. Mr. Pehle said
that he was familiar with these facts but that nevertheless
there were these large withdrawals required to be made from the
Yokohama Specie Bank for the purposesmentioned above and that
the bank authorities in New York were concerned that the rate
of withdrawal might so exceed the rate of realization on silk
bills that the condition of the bank might, at some time in the
future, become critical. Mr. Pehle suggested that Mr. Nisiyama
and the officials of the Yokohama Specie Bank might wish to
discuss this matter further with the New York State bank
authorities, and that Mr. Nisiyama might also wish to take up
with the officials of the bank the question of effecting re-
mittances to American missionaries in Japan against blocked
dollars. Mr. Nisiyama agreed with Mr. Pehle that this would be
a proper procedure for him to follow.
Toluller

MR. ACHESON
October 13, 1941
PRECLUSIVE BUYING OF LATIN AMERICAN COTTON
A Proliminary Survey
1. - At the present time Japan is not purchasing cotton
from the United States and India, the principal prewer sources of
supply for that commodity. Except for the Japanese-controlled areas
of China, the main sources of supply still open to Japan are in Latin
America, primarily Brazil and Peru. Because of this fact and the fact
that cotton is about the only important industrial raw material pro-
duced in Latin America which is not now being bought or to be bought
on a virtually preclusive basis, the ouestion arises as to the
desirability end feasibility of joint United States - United Kingdom
action to acquire available supplies of cotton in Latin Americen
countries.
2. - The attached table (Table I) shovs the estimated
exportable surpluses of cotton in all the significant surplus producing
countries of the world. This table shows thet for the cotton marketing
year beginning august 1 there is anparently available in the world a
total exportable supply of about 25, 400,000 bales of which the United
States accounts for 13,264,000 bales or 52 percent, the British Empire
and protected countries account for about 7,748,000 bales or 31
percent, and Latin American countries account for 4,164,000 bales or
slightly over 16 percent. The remaining one percent is accounted
for mainly by Turkey and certain Portuguese possessions.

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.98)
一2-
3. - Of the 4,160,000 bales of cotton estimated to be
available in Latin American countries it is anticipated that over
3,000,000 bales would be available for purchase by the United States
and the United Kingdom. This latter figure assumes that about
900,000 bales may have been sold by the various Latin American coun-
tries to other parts of the Western Hemisphere, such es Canada, and
to other parts of the world, such as Portugal and Spain, not under
Axis control. It also considers that the United Kingdom already owns
about 100,O00 bales of cotton in Brazil and & small but undetermined
quantity in Peru.
4. - The cost of the available stocks in Latin America during
the marketing year beginning August 1, l94l, to the United States and/
or the United Kingdom is estimated to be approximately 120 million
dollars. This estimate is based on the quantity of 3,000,000 beles
and an average cost per bale of $40.00. This latter figure is above
the government loan rates currently in effect in Brazil and Peru.
5. - While Latin American cotton is now about the only
cotton other than Chinese available to Japan, it appears that actions
already taken in the way of freezing of funds and the closing of the
Panama Canal to Japanese ships has for the time being at least ended
shipments of Brazilian cotton to Japan. No shinments of Brazilian
cotton to Japan have been reported since about August 21 and no ship-
ments to China (Shanghai) since about September 6. Japan is, however,
continuing to secure cotton in Peru.
6. - The need on the part of Japan for Latin Americen
cotton will not be as urgent as might be thought because of the stocks

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.98)
13-
of cotton available in that country as of August 1 and the fact that
the greater part of Japan's export trade in cotton goods has been
lost. It is now estimated that Japan had approximately 450,000
bales of cotton to carry into the marketing season beginning August 1,
as well as a stock of piece goods equivalent to 650,000 to 700,000
bales. It is also estimated thet an export trade in cotton goods
equivalent to 900,000 bales of cotton has been lost. The prewar
consumption of cotton in Japan (1933-37) averaged about 3,500,000
bales per year of which about 50 percent was used in the manufacture
of export goods. In the last year or two Japanese consumption of
cotton for domestic use has been running at the rate of only about
600,000 bales. Consequently, taking into account the enforced
curtailment in domestic consumption of cotton goods in Japan it is
believed that, by using up most of its available stocks and importing
some 300,000 or more bales of cotton from China, that country could
get along without any appreciable imports from sources of supply other
than China for something more than two years. On the other hand, it
is not to be supposed that Japan would wish so nearly to exhaust
supplies of raw cotton or thet Japan would not continue to buy cotton,
possibly even to enlarge stocks, if it were possible to do so.
7. - On the basis of the foregoing facts and considerations
the following conclusions are presented:
(a) that practically the entire "free" supply of
cotton in the world is in Latin America and that the acquisi-
tion of this quantity would cost in the neighborhood of
120 million dollars.

=== Page 442 === (confidence 0.99)
一4
(b) that the acquisition of this supply of cotton
by the United States and the United Kingdom would effectively
forestall any significant future manufacture in Japan of
cotton goods for export and would meke necessary continued
severe curtailment of production of cotton goods for domestic
consumption but it would not be an additional blow of
significant force to the general Japanese economy.
(c) that the acquisition of the "free" supply of
Latin American cotton would probably have more significance
in the sense of assisting an industry of primary importance
in at least two of these countries, Brazil and Peru, and at
the same time would virtually eliminate commercial intercourse
between Japan and these countries.
(d) that, in view of the world supply indicated
in paragraph number 1, any program of buying that might be
decided upon should be coupled with commitments to reduce,
or for the smaller countries, not to expand cotton production.

=== Page 448 === (confidence 0.98)
October 18, 1941
MEMORANDUM TO MR. BUNN
Yesterday at the meeting of the alternates on the
Economic Defense Board, Mr. Milo Perkins referred to a
report which came from the Treasury some time ago that
gasoline which we were exporting to Spain was being re-
exported to Germany. I told him that Mr. Long had made
some inquiries into this report and found that it orig1-
nated with a sailor on one of the Spanish ships. We did
not believe that sufficiont reliance could be put on the
report to warrant changing the present policy towards
Spain which had the strong support of the British Govern-
mont.
This led to some general inquiries by Mr. Perkins
as to our policy towards Spain. I told him that some
time ago I had asked for a resurvey of the Spanish policy
with a view toward making as complete a statement as
possible for the use of Mr. Yost and the Office of Export
Control. I be 1eve that a Committee composed of
Mr. Stinebower, Mr. Reinstein, and Mr. George from the
European Division went to work on this subject. Has any
written statement of policy eventuated? If so, could I
see it? If not, could we press forward with the watter?
Dean Achesom

Washington, D.C.
Sir:
I have the honor to enclose a translation of the
inward and outward freight manifests of the Japanese
FEB y -1
vessel "Toa Marú", which arrived in Rio de Janeiro on
FILED
Confidential File
October 29 and sailed for Japan via Buenos Aires on
November 1, 194l. This was the first Japanese vessel
to call at a Brazilian port singe August l94l
eemeg
Enclosure No. 1, which lists the exports of Brazil-
ian produots to Japan on this vessely rofleots both. the
strength

=== Page 468 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
strength and weaknesses of our preclusive purchasing
program in Brazil. It will be noted that no shipments
were made of materials contained in the United States-
Brazil Strategic Materials Agreement, although Japan
previous to June 1l had been a heavy purchaser of many
of these materials, particularly quartz crystal, indus-
trial diamonds and mica. The outward manifest does
reveal, however, that Japan's current purchases in Brazil
are almost entirely of armament materials not included
in our agreement.
The Brazilian authorities have informed me that they
took unusual precautions while the vessel was in port to
prevent materials included in our agreement from being
illegally placed on the ship.
It appears improbable that, in case of hostilities
between Japan and the United States, Japanese vessels would
call at Brazilian ports. It is more likely that Japan
will attempt to obtain Brazilian strategic materials via
original shipments to West Coast ports and transshipment
to Japan at those ports.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
J6hn F. Simmons,
Counselor of Embassy.

2. Translation inward manifest.

I have the honor to refer toVthe Legation's despatch
No. 1976 of September 8, 1941, with reference to Japanese
payments and collections in Bolivia through Chilean and
Peruvian banks, and to forward for the information of the
Department a copy of a cable addressed to the Banco Central
de Bolivia by the One Hundredth Bank Ltd., Yokohama, instruct-
ing the Banoo Central to remit the proceeds of its collections
for the Yokohama Bank to the Banao Popular del Perú, Lima.

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
The oable follows:
28/10/41
Banoo Central de Bolivia, La Paz.
"Please remit proceeds of all our collections to Banco
Popular del Perú, Lima, by airmail, instructing them
to advise and pay same amount to Banco Italiano Lima,
Perú, for account of Yokohama Specie Bank Ltd., Yokohama.
The One Hundredth Bank Ltd."
Yokohama.
Ih the meantime firms and individuals which have been
included in and recommended for the Proclaimed List of Blocked
Nationals continue from time to time purchasing dollar drafts
to the order of United States banks in payment for merchandise
imported cash against doouments. Three recent cases have
come to the attention of the Legetion.
The first involved a sale by the Banco Central on October
29 of a draft in the amount of $6,273.50 to A. SILES, a "palo
branoo" for Kyllmann Bauer. This draft was made payable to
the Bank of America and National Trust and Savings Association,
San Francisco, and covers the importation of sago and cummin
seed. In this connection, the Department's attention is
respectfully invited to the Legation's telegram No. 295
of October 17, 3 p.m., and its despatch No. 1230 of October
20, 1941, reporting a shipment of cummin seed to A. Siles by
Gerhard and Hooper, San Franoisco. Whether or not this
particular payment covers that transaction is not definitely
known but if not it indioates that Gerhard and Hooper may have
been carrying on business with Siles for some time past.
Interesting also in this regard is in rmation recently obtai ned
by the Legation that on August 8 of this year Kyllmann Bauer
y Cia. itself (it will be remembered that Siles acts as a front
for Kyllmann Bauer) purchased a draft in the amount of $3,648
to cover the importation of cloves and cinnamon from Gerhard and
Hooper.

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Hooper. The suspicion that the San Francisoo firm was
aotually aware of the true status of Siles thus seems
considerably strengthened since its shipments to him have
been of exactly the same kind of merohandise formerly
exported directly to Kyllmann Bauer.
The second payment of this kind of which the Legation
has knowledge is a draft dated November 3, 1941, in the
amount of $14.45 payable to the Manufacturers Trust Company,
New York, purchased by Kyllmann Bauer y Cia. from the
Banco Central de Bolivia and covering the importation of hat
samples. The name of the exporter is not known.
The third similar case of a payment to a United States
bank, not quite so recent but perhaps still of interest,
was covered by a draft purchased from the Banco Central
on September 23, 1941, by Juan GUMUCIO who, as reported in
the Legation's despatch No. 1115 of September 22, 1941, is
a front for Kyllmann Bauer. This draft was in the amount
of $5,450.03 and was for automobile tires and tubes. The
exporter of the merohandise is not definitely known but
was probably the Fisk Tire Company, since Kyllmann Bauer
has in the past been the representative for Bolivia of this
line.
Several other instances of dollar payments by blocked
be
nationals to what are believed to/American firms have also
come to the Legation's attention within the past weok. The
details of these transactions follow:
Draft dated August 2, 1941, in the amount of
%508.50; sold by the Banco Central to E. Sohilling
and made payable to George Leuder & Co. to cover
importation of "industrial essences".
Draft dated September 25, 1941, in the amount of
$174.76; sold by the Banoo Central to Juan Elsner & Cia.
and made payable to Simplioity Pattern Co. to cover
importation of patterns.
Draft

=== Page 480 === (confidence 0.96)

Draft dated August 1, 1941, in the amount of

to cover importation of linen material.
Whether any of these payments by listed firms indicate
that commeroial relations are being maintained with them by
United States concerns is not known, but the Department
may consider the matter worthy of further investigation.
